1 
CRIMINAL LAW  
 
 
I. FUNDAMENTAL PRINCIPLES 
 
 
A. CONSTRUCTION OR 
INTERPRETATION OF PENAL LAWS 
 
1. Effects of Repeal or Amendment 
 
Absolute Repeal 
 
This obliterates the crime defined and 
punished by the repealed law. The act is 
decriminalized, and the beneficial effects of 
the repeal are retroactively applied. This 
deprives the courts of the jurisdiction to try 
pending and future cases involving the 
decriminalized act. Persons convicted of the 
decriminalized act should be immediately 
released. 
 
Basis: Nullum crimen nulla poena sine lege 
 
Partial Repeal (a.k.a. Repeal with Re-
enactment; a.k.a. Amendment) 
 
The new law expressly repeals an old law, 
but it reenacts the act previously punished 
under the old law, albeit with some 
modifications 
on 
penalties, 
modifying 
circumstances, etc. 
 
In such case, the wrongful act continues to 
be punishable, but this time under the 
provisions of the new law. 
 
Amendment of Penal Laws 
 
a. Favorable 
to 
the 
accused 
- 
retroactively applied, except habitual 
delinquents 
b. Unfavorable to the accused - 
prospectively applied 
 
 
 
 
2. Retroactive Effect of Penal Laws 
 
GR: Penal laws have prospective effect 
(non-retroactive.) 
 
XPN: Penal laws shall be given retroactive 
effect: 
 
a. If the law is favorable to the accused 
who is not a habitual delinquent 
b. If the law decriminalizes an act 
(absolute repeal) 
c. If the law expressly provides for 
retroactivity, subject to Constitutional 
proscription against ex post facto laws. 
 
 
3. Pro Reo Principle or Rule of Lenity 
 
In case of doubt, penal statutes should be 
strictly construed against the State only when 
the law is ambiguous and there is doubt 
regarding its interpretation. 
 
If there is no doubt, penal laws should be 
strictly applied. 
 
EQUIPOISE RULE 
 
 
Under the equipoise rule, when evidence is 
equally 
balanced 
between 
guilt 
and 
innocence, the doubt must be resolved in 
favor of the accused. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 


<!-- PAGE 8 -->

 
 
2 
4. Suppletory Application of Revised 
Penal Code to Special Laws – RPC, Art. 
10 
Offenses which are or in the future may be 
punishable under special laws are not 
subject to the provisions of this Code. This 
Code shall be supplementary to such laws, 
unless the latter should specially provide the 
contrary. 
B. NULLUM CRIMEN, NULLA POENA 
SINE LEGE 
 
It literally means “there is no crime when 
there is no law punishing it.” Thus, while 
antisocial acts should be penalized, there 
must be a clear definition of the punishable 
offense as well as the penalty that may be 
imposed. 
 
C. MALA IN SE AND MALA PROHIBITA 
 
Crimes mala in se are those where the acts 
are inherently immoral; while crimes mala 
prohibita are those which become crimes 
because it is made so by law. 
 
Mala in se v. Mala Prohibita 
MALA IN SE 
MALA PROHIBITA 
As to Nature 
Wrong from its very 
nature 
Wrong 
merely 
because there is a law 
prohibiting the same 
As to Basis 
Moral state of the 
offender 
The voluntariness of 
the offender 
As to laws Violated 
Generally punishable 
by RPC 
Generally punishable 
by Special Laws 
As to Intent 
Requires criminal 
intent on the part of 
the offender. 
GR: mere intent to 
perpetrate the act or 
done 
freely 
and 
consciously 
is 
enough. 
 
XPN: 
Temporary, 
incidental, casual or 
harmless possession 
or control of a firearm 
is not a violation of a 
statute prohibiting the 
possessing 
or 
carrying of this kind of 
weapon. 
As to Use of Good Faith as a Defense 
Good faith or lack of 
criminal intent is a 
defense. 
Good faith or lack of 
criminal intent is NOT 
accepted 
as 
a 
defense, 
UNLESS 
this is an element of 
the crime such as in 
Sec. 3(e) of R.A. 3019 
[evident bad faith] or 
the law specifically 
provides. 
As to Modifying Circumstances (e.g. 
mitigating or aggravating circumstances) 
These circumstances 
are taken into account 
in 
imposing 
the 
penalty 
of 
the 
offender 
precisely 
because 
his moral 
trait is the basis of the 
crime. 
GR: 
These 
circumstances are not 
considered because 
the law intends to 
discourage 
the 
commission of the act 
specially prohibited. 
 
XPN: 
When 
the 
special law provides 
for such mitigating or 
aggravating 
circumstances. 
As to Degree of Participation 
Degree 
of 
participation 
(principal, 
Generally not taken 
into account; unless

into account in the

D. CARDINAL PRINCIPLES OF 
CRIMINAL LAW 
 
1. Generality 
 
Penal laws are obligatory on all persons who 
live or sojourn in Philippine territory, 
regardless of nationality, gender, or other 
personal circumstances, subject to the 
principles of public international law and to 
treaty stipulations (Art. 14, NCC).] 
 
XPNs: 
 
a. Treaty Stipulations. The Visiting Forces 
Agreement (VFA), which was signed on 
February 10, 1998, and came into force on 
June 1, 1999, is an agreement between the 
Philippine and US Government regarding the 
treatment of US Armed Forces visiting the 
Philippines (Bayan v. Zamora, G.R. No. 
138570 2000). 
 
 b.   Laws of Preferential Application  
 
Examples: 
 
i. RA 75 – penalizes acts which would impair 
proper observance by the Republic and its 
inhabitants of the immunities, rights and 
privileges 
of 
duly-accredited 
foreign 
diplomatic representatives in the Philippines; 
 
ii. Sec. 11 of Art. 6 of Constitution provides 
that no member of Congress shall be 
questioned nor be held liable in any other 
place for any speech or debate in the 
Congress or in any committee thereof. 
 
iii. Sec. 2 of Art. 11 of the Constitution 
provides that the President, the VP, the 
Supreme 
Court 
justices, 
members 
of 
Constitutional 
Commissions, 
and 
the 
Ombudsman may be removed from office on 
impeachment. 
 
 iv. RA 7055 provides for jurisdiction of 
civilian courts vis-à-vis the military courts. 
 
c.   Principles of Public International Law 
 Who are Exempt (SCAMMP) 
 
1.   Sovereigns and other heads of state 
2.   Charges d’ affaires 
3.   Ambassadors 
4.   Ministers 
5.   Minister resident 
6.   Plenipotentiary (Art. 14, Vienna 
Convention on Diplomatic Relations) 
  
Note: consuls, vice-consuls and other 
commercial 
representatives 
of 
foreign nations cannot claim the 
privileges and immunities accorded 
to ambassadors and ministers unless 
there is a specific treaty or executive 
agreement that gives them specific 
community 


<!-- PAGE 10 -->

 
 
4 
 
2.Territoriality 
 
GR: Philippine penal laws are enforceable 
only within its territory. 
 
XPN: When the penal law provides for 
extraterritorial application.  
 
Examples: 
 
a) Art. 2 of RPC 
b) Anti - VAWC, which punishes the 
offender even if he committed the act 
outside the Philippines, if the victim is in 
the Philippines at the time of the 
commission of the offense; 
c) Cybercrime Prevention Act, which 
punishes the offender even if he 
committed the act if the victim is a 
Filipino, or the victim is in the Philippines, 
or the damage is incurred in the 
Philippines, at the time of the commission 
of the offense; 
d) Anti-Terrorism Act 
 
3.Prospectivity 
 
GR: Penal laws should be enforced 
prospectively, meaning, a person could not 
be made liable for an act that was not 
punishable at the time of its commission. 
 
XPN: Penal laws shall be given retroactive 
effect: 
 
a. If the law is favorable to the accused who 
is not a habitual delinquent 
b. If the law decriminalizes an act (absolute 
repeal) 
c. If the 
law 
expressly 
provides for 
retroactivity. 
 
 
 
 
 
E. CONSTITUTIONAL LIMITATIONS ON 
THE POWER TO ENACT PENAL LAWS 
 
1. Equal Protection 
 
A person similarly situated should not be 
treated differently. Note that a statute may, 
on its face, be violative of the equal 
protection clause; in such case, the statute 
must 
be 
struck 
down 
for 
being 
unconstitutional. However, a statute may be 
nondiscriminatory on its fact, but may be 
discriminatory 
when 
applied 
and 
administered by public authority with an evil 
eye; in such case, the acts of such public 
authority may be corrected via certiorari. 
 
2. Due Process 
 
Due process, in essence, requires notice 
and 
opportunity to be 
heard. 
Notice 
requirement 
includes 
the 
publication 
requirement under Art. 2 of NCC. 
 
A law which hears before it condemns; which 
proceeds 
upon 
inquiry, 
and 
renders 
judgment only after trial. Due process 
contemplates notice and opportunity to be 
heard before judgment is rendered, affecting 
one’s person or property. It is designed to 
secure justice as a living reality; not to 
sacrifice it by paying undue homage to 
formality. For substance must prevail over 
form. (Albert vs. CFI of Manila, 23 SCRA 
948). 
 
3. Bill of Attainder 
 
 
A bill of attainder is a legislative act that 
inflicts punishment without trial, its essence 
being the substitution of legislative fiat for a 
judicial determination of guilt. 


<!-- PAGE 11 -->

 
 
5 
 
4. Ex Post Facto Law 
 
An ex post facto law is a law that either: 
 
(1) makes criminal an act done before the 
passage of the law that was innocent when 
done, and punishes such act; or 
 
 
(2) aggravates a crime, or makes the crime 
greater than it was when committed; or 
 
 
(3) changes the punishment and inflicts a 
greater punishment than the law annexed 
to the crime when it was committed; or 
 
 
 
 
(4) alters the legal rules of evidence, and 
authorizes conviction upon less or different 
testimony than the law required at the time of 
the commission of the offense; or 
 
 
 
(5) assumes to regulate civil rights and 
remedies only, but in effect imposes a 
penalty or deprivation of a right for an act 
that was lawful when done; or 
 
 
 
(6) deprives a person accused of a crime of 
some lawful protection to which he has 
become entitled, such as the protection of a 
former 
conviction 
or 
acquittal, 
or 
a 
proclamation of amnesty. (Fr. Buenafe et al.  
v. BBM et al. (G.R. No. 260374. June 28, 
2022, En banc) 
 
 
5. Excessive Fines, Cruel, 
Degrading, or Inhuman 
Punishment 
 
Excessive fines shall not be imposed, nor 
cruel, degrading or inhuman punishment 
inflicted. Neither shall death penalty be 
imposed, unless, for compelling reasons 
involving heinous crimes, the Congress 
hereafter provides for it. Any death penalty 
already imposed shall be reduced to 
reclusion perpetua. 
 
Cruelty refers to physical suffering of the 
victim purposely intended by the offender. 
(People vs. Gatcho,. 103 SCRA 207) 
 
The employment of physical, psychological, 
or degrading 
punishment 
against 
any 
prisoner 
or 
detainee 
or 
the 
use 
of 
substandard or inadequate penal facilities 
under subhuman conditions shall be dealt 
with by law. (Section 19, Article III of the 1987 
Constitution)  


<!-- PAGE 12 -->

 
 
6 
 
 
II. FELONIES AND CRIMINAL LIABILITY 
RPC Book 1 and Related Laws 
 
 
 
A. FELONIES 
 
1. Definition 
 
Felony refers exclusively to acts or omissions 
punished under the RPC. Violations of 
special laws are generally referred to as 
offenses.  
 
It is necessary to know whether a crime is a 
felony or an offense. For instance, in Article 
160, a convicted prisoner who commits a 
felony while serving sentence is liable for 
quasi-recidivism in addition to the felony. But 
if he commits an offense while serving such 
sentence, he is not liable for recidivism for 
the article uses the word "felony”. 
 
2.Dolo and Culpa 
 
Dolo  
(Intentional 
Felony) 
Culpa 
(Unintentional 
Felony) 
As to Intent 
With 
deliberate 
intent. 
Injury caused is 
unintentional, 
it 
being an incident 
of another act 
performed 
without malice. 
As to the Source of Wrong 
Committed 
Has intention to 
cause a wrong. 
Wrongful 
act 
results 
from 
imprudence, 
negligence, lack 
of foresight, or 
lack of skill. 
 
3. Felony and Crime, 
Distinguished 
Felony - committed not only by means of 
deceit (dolo), but likewise by means of 
fault (culpa). There is deceit when the 
wrongful act is performed with deliberate 
intent; and there is fault when the wrongful 
act results from imprudence, negligence, 
lack of foresight or lack of skill. 
Crime - umbrella term covering (a) felonies 
under the RPC and (b) offenses under 
special penal laws.  
4. Gravity 
Classifications of Felonies According to 
their Gravity   
1. Grave - those to which the law attaches 
the capital punishment or penalties which in 
any of their periods are afflictive, in 
accordance with Art. 25 of the RPC. (Art. 9 
(1), RPC)  
2. Less Grave - those which the law 
punishes with penalties which in their 
maximum 
period 
are 
correctional, 
in 
accordance with Art. 25 of the RPC. (Art. 
9(2), RPC)  
Note: The criminal can still be rehabilitated 
and hence can be the subject of probation 
and Alternative Dispute Resolution (ADR) 
insofar as the civil aspect is concerned.  
3. Light - those infractions of law for the 
commission of which the penalty of arresto 
menor or a fine not exceeding P40,000 
pesos, or both, is provided. (Art. 9(3), RPC, 
as amended by R.A. No. 10951, 29 Aug. 
2017) 
 


<!-- PAGE 13 -->

 
 
7 
 
5. Stages of Execution 
 
a. Subjective Phase and Objective 
Phase 
Subjective Phase 
That portion of the acts constituting the crime 
included between the act which begins the 
commission of the crime and the last act 
performed by the offender which, with prior 
acts, should result in the consummated 
crime. After that point has been breached, 
the subjective phase ends and the objective 
phase begins. If the offender never passes 
the subjective phase of the offense, the crime 
is merely attempted. 
 
On the other hand, the subjective phase is 
completely passed in case of frustrated 
crimes, for in such instances, subjectively, 
the crime is complete. (Valenzuela v. 
Natividad, G.R. No. 160188, June 25, 2007) 
 
Objective Phase 
 
It starts from the time the offender has 
performed all acts necessary to accomplish 
the crime because there is nothing more he 
needed to do to accomplish his crime. 
 
The crime does not depend anymore on the 
subject. The acts done will now run its own 
course and would result in either frustrated 
felony or to consummated felony. 
 
b. Spontaneous Desistance 
 
In the attempted stage, the offender has not 
yet performed all acts of execution. To be 
absolved from criminal liability, he must 
spontaneously desist from performing all 
acts of execution. 
 
“Spontaneous” is not equivalent to voluntary. 
The term “spontaneous” means proceeding 
from natural feeling or native tendency 
without external constraint; it is synonymous 
with impulsive, automatic and mechanical 
(People vs. Lizada, G.R. No. 143468-71, 
January 24, 2003, En Banc). 
 
Factors in the cause of the non-
performance of all acts of execution 
 
i. If the cause of non-performance of all acts 
of execution is spontaneous desistance - not 
liable. 
 
Note: In an attempted homicide case, 
the accused will be absolved from 
criminal liability for homicide if he 
desisted from inflicting mortal wound 
upon the victim. However, he is liable 
for the crime that he already 
completed such as physical injuries, 
threat, or discharge of firearm. 
 
ii. If the cause of the non-performance of all 
the acts of execution is other than the 
offender's spontaneous desistance, the 
felony is attempted. 
 
c. Attempted, Frustrated, and 
Consummated Stages 
 
i. Attempted Felony 
 
When 
the 
offender 
commences 
the 
commission of a felony directly by overt acts 
but does not produce the felony by reason of 
some cause or accident other than his own 
spontaneous desistance. 
 


<!-- PAGE 14 -->

 
 
8 
 
ii. Frustrated Felony 
 
When the offender performs all the acts of 
execution which would produce the felony 
but does not produce it by reason of causes 
independent of the will of the perpetrator. 
 
 
Crimes Which Do Not Admit of Frustrated 
Stage (RABT) 
 
 
a) Rape - Once there is penetration, no 
matter how slight it is, the offense is 
consummated [People v. Orita, G.R. No. 
88724 (1990)]. 
 
b) Arson - The crime of arson is already 
consummated even if only a portion of 
the wall or any part of the house is 
burned. The consummation of the crime 
does not depend upon the extent of the 
damage caused [People v. Hernandez, 
G.R. No. L- 31770 (1929)]. 
 
c) Bribery and Corruption of Public 
Officers - If there is a meeting of the 
minds, there is consummated bribery or 
consummated corruption [US v. Basa, 8 
Phil. 89 (1907)]. 
  
d) Theft  - Once there is unlawful taking, 
theft is consummated. It does not matter 
how long the property was in the 
possession of the accused; it does not 
matter 
whether 
the 
property 
was 
disposed of or not [Valenzuela v. 
People, G.R. No. 160188 (2007)]. 
 
iii. Consummated Felony 
 
When all the elements necessary for its 
execution and accomplishment are present; 
the felony is produced. 
 
Crimes without Frustrated and Attempted 
Stages (Only Consummated): 
 
1. Offenses punishable by Special Penal 
Laws, unless the law provides otherwise 
 
2. Formal crimes - consummated in one 
instance  
Ex: slander, adultery, etc. 
 
3. Impossible Crimes 
 
4. Crimes consummated by mere attempt 
 
Ex: attempt to flee to an enemy 
country, 
treason, 
corruption 
of 
minors) 
 
5. Felonies by omission 
 
6. Crimes committed by mere agreement 
 
Ex: betting in sports: “ending,” 
corruption of public officers) 
 
d. Preparatory and Overt Acts 
 
It consists of physical activity or deed, 
indicating the intention to commit a particular 


<!-- PAGE 15 -->

 
 
9 
crime, more than a mere planning or 
preparation, which if carried out to its 
complete termination following its natural 
course, without being frustrated by external 
obstacles 
nor 
by 
the 
spontaneous 
desistance of the perpetrator, will logically 
and necessarily ripen into a concrete offense 
(Rait v. People, G.R. No. 180425 (2008). 
 
6. Plurality of Crimes 
 
Plurality of Crimes exists when the actor 
commits various delictual acts of the same or 
different kind. There are two kinds of 
plurality, material (real) plurality and formal 
(ideal) plurality of crimes. 
 
Real or Material 
Ideal or Formal 
A 
person 
who 
performs two or more 
criminal acts one after 
the other is liable for 
every 
criminal 
act 
committed since each 
crime is motivated by a 
separate 
criminal 
intent. In such case, 
more than one crime 
is committed. 
Although two or more 
crimes are actually 
committed, 
they 
constitute only one 
crime in the eyes of 
the law as well as in 
the conscience of the 
offender. 
Ex. Multiple counts of 
a certain crime 
Ex. 
Robbery 
with 
Homicide 
 
a. Absorption Principle 
 
In this principle, although there have been 
multiple violations, that crime that is 
ascertained to be an inherent part, an 
element, or that is made in furtherance of the 
other crime is not treated as a separate 
offense but is deemed included in the 
other crime. 
 
Under this doctrine, the crime for which the 
offender originally or primarily intended to 
commit absorbs the offense which is 
executed in its furtherance. 
The Court has applied the doctrine of 
absorption in the crime of rebellion, such that 
common crimes (e.g., murder, homicide, 
arson) and offenses under special laws, 
when committed as necessary means or in 
furtherance of the political offense, are 
absorbed in rebellion and are not punished 
as separate crimes. 
To illustrate: 
If a group of rebels kills a government soldier 
during an armed encounter to advance their 
uprising, the killing (homicide or murder) is 
absorbed in the crime of rebellion, because it 
is committed in furtherance of the political 
objective. 
However, if during the same incident a rebel 
rapes a civilian, the crime of rape is NOT 
absorbed, because rape is not a necessary 
means nor an act in furtherance of 
rebellion. It is committed for personal 
gratification, not to advance the political 
purpose, and is therefore punishable as a 
separate offense. 
Note: The theory of absorption in 
rebellion cases must not confine itself 
to common crimes but also to 
offenses under special laws which 
are perpetrated in furtherance of the 
political offense. [Enrile v. Amin 
(1990)] 
 
b. Single Impulse Rule 
 
It is the taking of several things, whether 
belonging to the same or different owners, at 


<!-- PAGE 16 -->

 
 
10 
the same time and place constitutes but one 
larceny. 
(Defensor 
Santiago 
v. 
Garchitorena, GR 109266. Dec. 2, 1993). 
  
Examples: 
  
i. Insurrection, Rebellion, and other 
crimes and offenses committed in 
furtherance, on the occasion thereof, 
or incident thereto. (In the matter of 
petition for Habeas Corpus of Umil, 
G.R. NO. 81567, 1990) 
 
ii. Eight robberies as component parts of 
a general plan. (Pp v. Dela Cruz, G.R. 
NO. L-1745) 
 
Single Larceny Doctrine is not applicable: 
 
a. BP 22 Cases (Lim vs. People, G.R. No. 
14321, October 26, 2001) 
 
b. Killing or causing injury through the 
use of firearm. The SC declared that it is 
not the act of pressing the trigger which 
should produce the several felonies, but 
the number of bullets which actually 
produced 
them. 
Hence, 
where 
the 
accused 
pressed 
the 
trigger 
of 
a 
submachine gun and the gun fired 
continually and several persons killed or 
injured, there are as many crimes as are 
persons killed or injured. (People vs. 
Sanchez, G.R. No. 131116, August 29, 
1999) 
 
c. Compound Complex Crime 
A compound crime exists when a single act 
results in two or more grave or less grave 
felonies. The law treats the multiple felonies 
as one complex crime, and only one penalty 
is imposed, albeit in its maximum period. 
The focus is not on the number of victims or 
crimes, but on the number of acts. 
If only one physical act was committed, but 
that one act produced multiple serious 
felonies, the law considers it unjust to punish 
the offender separately for each felony 
Requisites: 
 
1. That only a Single act is performed by the 
offender 
 
2. That the single act produces: 
 
a. 2 or more Grave Felonies; or 
b. 1 or more Grave and 1 or more Less 
Grave Felonies; or 
c. 2 or more Less Grave Felonies. 
Note: Only grave and less grave felonies are 
counted for compound crimes. The law 
deliberately excludes light felonies because 
they are minor offenses. Including them 
would defeat the policy of proportionality of 
punishment. Thus, there is NO compound 
crime involving light felonies. 
Light felonies produced by the same act 
should be treated and punished as separate 
offenses or may be absorbed by the grave 
felony. (People vs. Turla, G.R. No. 26388, 
February 14, 1927) 
Examples: 
 
i. A single bullet killing two persons 
 One trigger pull 
 Two deaths 
 Two grave felonies (two homicides or 
murders) 
ii. The act of raping a girl, causing her 
physical injuries which required 
medical attention for about 20 days. 
(Considered a complex crime of rape 
with less serious physical injuries) 
 
 One act of rape 
 Resulted in rape (grave felony) 


<!-- PAGE 17 -->

 
 
11 
 Also resulted in less serious physical 
injuries (less grave felony) 
 
d. Complex Crime Proper 
 
There is complex crime when an offense is a 
necessary means for committing another 
offense. It is only one crime as contemplated 
by law because the offender has only one 
criminal intent. 
 
Requisites:  
 
a. That at least Two offenses are 
committed. 
 
b. That One or Some of the offenses 
must be Necessary to commit the 
other; 
 
c. That both or all the offenses must 
be punished under the Same statute. 
 
Note: 
“Necessary” 
does 
NOT 
mean 
“indispensable” 
Arias v. People, G.R. Nos. 237106-07 
When the offender commits on a public, 
official or commercial document any of the 
acts of falsification enumerated in Article 171 
of the RPC as a necessary means to commit 
another crime like Estafa under Article 315 of 
the RPC, the two crimes form a complex 
crime under Article 48 of the same law.  
 
In a complex crime of Estafa Through 
Falsification of Public, Official or Commercial 
Document, the falsified document is actually 
utilized to defraud another. The falsification 
is already consummated and it is the 
defraudation which causes damage or 
prejudice to another that constitutes estafa. 
 
 
 
No 
Complex 
Crime 
Proper 
in 
the 
following: 
 
1. Subsequent acts of intercourse, 
after forcible abduction with rape, are 
separate acts of rape. 
 
2. When trespass to dwelling is a 
direct means to commit a grave 
offense. 
 
3. When one offense is committed to 
conceal the other. 
 
4. Where one of the offenses is 
penalized by special law. 
 
5. There is no complex crime of 
rebellion with murder, arson, robbery, 
or other common. 
6. In case of continuous crimes. 
 
7. When the other crime is an 
indispensable element of the other 
offense. 
 
General Rules in Complex Crimes 
 
1. Information - only one (1) shall be filed. 
 
2. Jurisdiction - When two crimes produced 
by a single act are respectively within the 
exclusive jurisdiction of two courts of different 
jurisdiction, the court of higher jurisdiction 
shall try the complex crime. 
 
3. Penalty 
 
a) Where both penalties provide for 
imprisonment - penalty to be imposed 
is the penalty for the most serious 
crime, applied in its maximum. 
 
b) Where one of the penalties is 
imprisonment and the other is fine - 
only the penalty of imprisonment 
should be imposed. 
 


<!-- PAGE 18 -->

 
 
12 
4. When the other offense is not proven - 
accused can be convicted of the other. 
 
e. Special Complex Crime or 
Composite Crimes 
 
In substance, two or more crimes are 
committed; however, in the eyes of the law, 
there is only one crime.  
 
 
 
The law treats the combination as a single, 
indivisible, 
and 
unique 
offense 
and 
prescribes a single, specific penalty therefor. 
 
 
 
Special complex crimes do not fall under 
Article 48. They exist because the law itself 
expressly 
defines 
and 
penalizes 
the 
combination as one crime. 
 
Requisites: 
 
a. 2 or more crimes are committed 
b. But the law treats them as a Single, 
Indivisible, and unique offense 
c. Product of Single Criminal Impulse 
 
Characteristics 
 
1. The crime is indivisible; it cannot be 
split into separate offenses. 
 
2. The offender is punished under the 
specific provision of law, not under 
Article 48. 
 
3. The penalty is fixed by law, not 
determined 
by 
comparing 
the 
seriousness 
of 
the 
component 
crimes. 
 
4. Even if the additional crime was not 
intended, the special complex crime 
still 
exists 
once 
the 
statutory 
conditions are met. 
 
Examples: 
 
i. Robbery with Homicide, Art. 294 (1). 
ii. Robbery with Rape, Art. 294 (2). 
iii. Kidnapping with serious physical 
injuries, Art. 267 (3). 
iv. Rape with Homicide, Article 266-B, as 
amended by R.A. No. 8353. 
 
f. Continuous or Continuing Crime 
 
A continued (or continuous) crime exists 
when a series of acts are committed, each of 
which would ordinarily constitute a crime by 
itself, but the law treats them as only one 
crime because the acts are united by a single 
criminal resolution, and violate the same 
penal provision. 
 
Although there are plural acts, they are 
considered partial executions of a single 
criminal intent, and thus constitute only one 
offense in the eyes of the law. 
 
Requisites: 
 
a. Plurality of acts (a series of acts 
are committed) 
 
b. Unity of penal provision violated 
(the acts violate the same provision of 
law) 
 
c. Unity of criminal intent or single 
criminal resolution 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 


<!-- PAGE 19 -->

 
 
13 
 
People of the Philippines, et al. v. Ramoy, 
et al., G.R. No. 212738 
 
A continuing crime is also referred to as a 
transitory crime. Jurisprudence on the matter 
instructs that a continuing crime has two 
(2) variations.  
 
In the first, there are series of acts each 
material and essential to the crime that occur 
in different locations. In which case, the court 
where any of the essential ingredients of the 
crime took place has the jurisdiction to try the 
case. For instance, in the crime of estafa, the 
element of deceit takes place where a 
worthless check is issued and delivered, 
which may be different from where the 
damage was inflicted upon the check's 
dishonor by the drawee bank.  
 
Under the second type, all the elements 
occurred in a single place but the very nature 
of the offense committed is such that the 
violation of the law is deemed continuing. An 
example of this is the crime of libel where the 
libelous matter is published or circulated from 
one province to another. 
 
 
B. CRIMINAL LIABILITY 
 
Criminal liability shall be incurred: 
 
a. By any person committing a felony (delito) 
although the wrongful act done be 
different from that which he intended. 
 
 
 
 
 
b. By any person performing an act which 
would be an offense against persons or 
property, were it not for the inherent 
impossibility of its accomplishment or an 
account of the employment of inadequate 
or ineffectual means. (See discussion on 
Impossible Crime under “II-B.4. Impossible 
Crime”). 
 
One who commits an intentional felony is 
responsible for all the consequences which 
may naturally and logically result therefrom, 
whether foreseen or intended or not (People 
v. Herrera, G.R. Nos. 140557-58) 
 
1. Actus Reus and Mens Rea 
 
Actus reus refers to the criminal act; it is that 
overt act which consummates the crime. (Ex. 
killing; robbing; vexing) 
 
Mens rea refers to the criminal intent, or 
guilty mind, or wrongful purpose. “Actus non 
facit reum, nisi mens sit rea” means that an 
act is not criminal if the mind of the actor is 
not criminal. 
 
For conviction of intentional felonies, there 
must be a concurrence of the two - actus reus 
(the overt act that is punished by law) and 
mens rea (guilty mind or criminal intent). 
 
 
2.Criminal Causation 
 
a. Proximate Cause 
 
It refers to one which in natural sequence, 
undisturbed by an independent cause, 
produces the result complained of. The 
result, however, must be the natural probable 
consequence which ought to have been 
foreseen is likely to flow from the act 
complained of. (Sadaya vs. Try Me Taxi, Inc., 
54540-R, July 23, 1979.) 
 


<!-- PAGE 20 -->

 
 
14 
 
 
 
b. Efficient Intervening Cause 
 
An efficient intervening cause is one that 
destroys the causal connection between the 
negligent act and injury and thereby negates 
liability (Morril vs. Morril, 60 ALR 102).  
 
It must break the continuity of causal 
connection between the original negligent 
act or omission and the injury so that the 
former cannot be said to have been the 
efficient cause of the latter. 
 
3.Aberratio Ictus, Error In Personae, 
and Praeter Intentionem 
 
a. Aberratio Ictus (mistake in the blow) 
 
A person is criminally liable for committing an 
intentional felony although the actual victim 
is different from the intended victim due to 
mistake of blow. The circumstance of 
aberratio ictus can neither exempt the 
accused from criminal responsibility nor 
mitigate it. 
 
Jurisprudence on Aberratio Ictus 
 
Stray bullets, obviously, kill indiscriminately 
and often without warning, precluding the 
unknowing victim from repelling the attack or 
defending himself. At the outset, A had no 
intention to kill B, much less, employ any 
particular means of attack. Logically, B's 
death was random and unintentional and the 
method used to kill her, as she was killed by 
a stray a bullet, was, by no means, 
deliberate. Nonetheless, A is guilty of the 
death of B under Article 4 of the Revised 
Penal Code, pursuant to the doctrine 
of aberratio ictus, which imposes criminal 
liability for the acts committed in violation of 
law and for all the natural and logical 
consequences resulting therefrom. While it 
may not have been A's intention to shoot B, 
this fact will not exculpate him. B's death 
caused by the bullet fired by A was the 
natural and direct consequence of A's 
felonious deadly assault against Cabiedes. 
[People v. Adriano (G.R. No. 205228, July 
15, 2015)] 
 
b. Error in personae (mistake of identity) 
 
A person is criminally liable for committing an 
intentional felony, although the actual victim 
is different from the intended victim due to a 
mistake of identity. 
 
Jurisprudence on Error in personae 
 
Police officers, acting on information to arrest 
a notorious criminal “dead or alive,” shot and 
killed a man they mistakenly believed to be 
the fugitive while he was asleep and 
unarmed, without making any reasonable 
inquiry to verify his identity and despite the 
absence of resistance or aggression. The 
Court held that mistake of fact excuses 
criminal liability only when the mistake is 
made without fault or negligence and under 
circumstances compelling immediate action; 
where officers have time and opportunity to 
verify facts, the mistake is culpable.  
 


<!-- PAGE 21 -->

 
 
15 
Error in personae (mistake of identity) does 
not absolve liability when the killing is 
intentional and the error results from 
carelessness, consistent with Article 4 of the 
Revised Penal Code, which imposes liability 
even if the wrongful act done differs from 
what 
was 
intended. 
[People 
of 
the 
Philippines v. Oanis, et al. (G.R. No. 47722, 
1943)] 
 
c. Praeter Intentionem 
 
A person is criminal liable for committing an 
intentional felony although its wrongful 
consequence is graver than that intended. 
 
Effect of praeter intentionem: mitigating 
circumstance of lack of intent to commit so 
grave a wrong under Art. 13 of RPC. 
 
Jurisprudence on Praeter Intentionem 
 
Domingo Ural, a police officer on guard duty, 
maltreated a detention prisoner, Felix 
Napola, by repeatedly boxing him, stepping 
on his body, and later pouring a flammable 
substance on him and setting him on fire 
inside the jail cell. Napola sustained 
extensive burns and eventually died weeks 
later due to complications. Ural denied 
responsibility, claiming the victim’s clothes 
merely caught fire accidentally.  
 
The Supreme Court affirmed the conviction 
but appreciated the mitigating circumstance 
of praeter intentionem under Article 13(3) of 
the Revised Penal Code. The Court held that 
although Ural intentionally committed a 
felony and is liable under Article 4 for the 
resulting death, the evidence showed that he 
had no intent to kill; his design was merely to 
maltreat 
the 
prisoner. 
The 
graver 
consequence of death was beyond his 
criminal design, even if it was the natural and 
logical result of his acts. [People v. Ural (G.R. 
No. L-30801, March 27, 1974)] 
 
 
4.Impossible Crime 
 
The requisites of an impossible crime are: 
 
(1) that the act performed would be 
an 
offense 
against persons 
or 
property; 
(2) that the act was done with evil 
intent; and 
(3) That its accomplishment was 
inherently impossible, or the means 
employed was either inadequate or 
ineffectual.  
 
The crime committed is impossible 
crime if the offense sought to be 
committed is factually or legally 
impossible (People vs. Callao, G.R. 
No. 228945, March 14, 2018). 
 
Factual & Legal Impossibility 
 
1. Factual Impossibility - occurs when 
extraneous circumstances unknown to the 
actor or beyond his control prevent the 
consummation of the intended crime. 
 
Examples: 
 
Putting the hand inside an empty 
pocket with intention to steal a wallet 
is impossible crime because of 
factual impossibility (Intod vs. Court 
of 
Appeals, 
G.R. 
No. 
103119, 
October 21, 1992). 
 
If the check is unfunded, stealing the 
check and presenting it for payment 
with 
the 
bank 
constitute 
an 
impossible crime. It is factually 
impossible to accomplish the crime of 


<!-- PAGE 22 -->

 
 
16 
qualified theft since the check is 
unfunded (Jacinto vs. People, G.R. 
No. 162540, July 13, 2009). 
 
2. Legal Impossibility - occurs where the 
intended acts, even if completed, would not 
amount to a crime. Legal impossibility would 
apply to those circumstances where: 
 
(a) the motive, desire and expectation 
is to perform an act in violation of the 
law; 
 
(b) there is intention to perform the 
physical act; 
 
(c) there is a performance of the 
intended physical act; 
 
(d) the consequence resulting from 
the intended act does not amount to 
a crime. (Intod v. CA, supra). 
 
Example:  
 
Killing a dead person is an impossible 
crime because of legal impossibility. 
 
 
5.Duty of Courts when Act is Non-
punishable or Penalty is Excessive 
 
For acts borne out of a case which is not 
punishable by law and the court finds it 
proper to repress, the remedy is to render the 
proper decision and thereafter, report to the 
Chief Executive, through the DOJ, the 
reasons why the same act should be the 
subject of penal legislation. 
 
The premise here is that a deplorable act is 
present but is not the subject of any penal 
legislation, thus, the court is tasked to inform 
the Chief Executive of the need to make that 
act punishable by law through legislation. 
 
The court can likewise recommend to the 
Chief Executive to grant executive clemency 
in view of the harshness of the law such as 
in the case of the mother convicted with 
forcible abduction with rape with her son, and 
meted reclusion perpetua because of her 
maternal love which moved her to conspire 
with him. 
 
6. Participation in Acts Giving Rise to 
Criminal Liability 
 
a. Principals, Accomplices, and 
Accessories 
 
When more than one person participated in 
the commission of the felony, the law looks 
into their participation.  
 
I. Principals (Article 17) 
 
i. 
Those who take a direct part in 
the execution of the act (by direct 
participation); 
 
ii. 
Those who directly force or 
induce others to commit it (by 
inducement); 
 
iii. 
Those who cooperate in the 
commission of the offense by 
another act without which it 
would 
not 
have 
been 
accomplished 
(indispensable 
cooperation). 
 
 
 
 
 


<!-- PAGE 23 -->

 
 
17 
Three kinds of principals 
  
i. Principal by direct participation 
 
 Two or more persons taking part in the 
commission of a crime are considered 
principals by direct participation if the 
following requisites are present: 
 
(1) they participated in the criminal 
resolution and 
 
(2) they carried out their plan and 
personally took part in its execution 
by acts which directly tended to the 
same end. (People of the Philippines 
vs. Francisco Dacillo, G. R. No. 
149368, April 14, 2004) 
   
ii. Principal by inducement 
 
Conviction of a person as a principal by 
inducement requires: 
 
 (1) that the inducement be made with 
the 
intention 
of 
procuring 
the 
commission of the crime; and 
 
(2) that such inducement be the 
determining cause of the commission 
by the material executor. (Ambagan, 
Jr. vs. People, G.R. Nos. 204481-82,  
Oct. 14, 2015) 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Two (2) types of inducement that reduces 
the offender to a mere instrument; the 
person induced is exempt from liability 
  
a. Irresistible Force 
 
 
Such physical force as would produce an 
effect upon the individual that despite all his 
resistance, it reduces him to a mere 
instrument. 
 
Force, fear, duress, or intimidation must be 
present, imminent, impending; and of such 
nature as to induce a well-grounded 
apprehension of death or serious bodily harm 
if the act be done (People v. Anod, G.R. No. 
186420, 2009) 
  
b. Uncontrollable Fear 
 
Such fear that must be Serious, Actual, 
Grave  and of such kind that majority of men 
would succumb to such moral compulsion. 
Mere threat of future injury is not sufficient. 
Compulsion must be such that it reduced 
the offender to a mere instrument acting 
not only without will but against his will as 
well. It must be of such character to leave the 
accused no opportunity for escape (Ty v. 
People, G.R. No. 149275, 2004). 


<!-- PAGE 24 -->

 
 
18 
 
Where the words uttered did not make any 
great dominance or influence on the 
offenders or is no longer necessary as the 
offenders were already determined to 
commit the offending acts, the utterance will 
not make the utterer an inducer. (People v. 
Parungao, G.R. No. 125812, November 
28,1996) 
  
iii. Principal by Indispensable Cooperation 
  
The requisites for criminal liability under Art. 
17, par. 3, Rev. Penal Code are: 
 
(1) participation in the criminal 
resolution, i.e., there is either 
anterior conspiracy or unity of 
criminal purpose and intention 
immediately before or simultaneously 
with the commission of the crime 
charged; and 
 
(2) cooperation in the commission of 
the offense by performing another act 
without which it would not have been 
accomplished. (People v. Guevarra, 
GR-65017, November 13, 1989, 179 
SCRA 333) 
 
II. Accomplice (Article 18) 
 
Punishable Acts 
 
Accomplices are those persons who, not 
being included in Article 17, cooperate in 
the execution of the offense by previous or 
simultaneous acts. 
 
 
In other words: 
 
An accomplice cooperates in the execution 
of the offense by previous or simultaneous 
acts, provided he has no direct participation 
in its execution or does not force or induce 
others to commit it, or his cooperation is not 
indispensable to its accomplishment (People 
v. Mandolado, GR No. L-51304-05 (1983). 
 
 
Knowledge of Actual Crime Not Required 
 
One can be an accomplice even if he did not 
know of the actual crime intended by the 
principal, provided he was aware that it was 
an illicit act (People v. Doctolero, G.R. No. 
34386, 1991). 
 
Accomplice in Bigamy 
 
A person, whether man or woman, who 
knowingly consents or agrees to be married 
to another already bound in lawful wedlock is 
guilty as an accomplice in the crime of 
bigamy [Santiago v. People, G.R. No. 
200233 (2015)]. 
  
Liability of Accomplice 
 
The liability of accomplices is less than 
principals and they shall be meted with the 
penalty next lower in degree (1 degree) than 
that prescribed by law (Arts. 52, 54, & 56, 
RPC). 
 
It is also possible for an accomplice to be 
liable as such for a different crime than what 
the principal committed, such as when 
he/she provides transport for someone 
committing robbery but does not participate 


<!-- PAGE 25 -->

 
 
19 
in the killing committed by the latter [People 
v. Doble, G.R. No. L-30028, 1982). 
  
Lookout 
 
A lookout who was not part of the 
conspiracy but participated only after such 
decision was reached incurs criminal 
liability as an accomplice since he is 
merely an instrument of the crime and he 
cooperates after the decision to commit the 
same had already been made. (People v. De 
Vera, G.R. No. 128966, August 18, 1999) 
  
Accomplices are penalized one degree 
lower than the principal unless the law 
provides otherwise. 
  
III. Accessories (Article 19) 
 
 
 
 
Punishable Acts 
 
a. Have knowledge of the commission 
of the crime; 
b. Have not participated therein; 
and 
 
c.  Take part subsequent to its 
commission (accessory after 
the fact) in any of three 
manners: 
  
i. By profiting or assisting the 
offender to profit from the effects of 
the crime 
 
 
ii.   By concealing or destroying the 
body of the crime (corpus delicti) or 
effects or the instruments thereof to 
prevent its discovery; or 
 
 
iii.  By harboring, concealing or 
assisting (HAC)  in the escape of the 
principal: 
 
 
a. In case of a public officer for any crime if 
he acts with abuse of public functions; 
 
b. In case of private individuals when the 
principal is guilty of: [TAE Mu Parri] 
 


<!-- PAGE 26 -->

 
 
20 
Treason, Attempt on the life of the chief 
Executive, Murder, Parricide or is known to 
be habitually guilty of some other crime (not 
habitual delinquent which covers specific 
crime whereas accessory participates in 
"some other crime"). 
 
Police Officer as an accessory to the 
crime 
 
A police officer who was present when the 
crime was committed abused his official 
function when he failed to immediately 
arrest the offender and conduct a speedy 
investigation of the crime, but instead left the 
scene together with the offender, thus 
assisting the offender to escape. Being a 
public officer, he is an accessory to the 
crime. (People v. Antonio, July 14, 2000)

SAD SiR Af 
 
The 
offender's 
Spouse, 
Ascendant,

brothers and sisters (SIblings) or Relatives 
by AFfinity within the same degree are 
exempt from criminal liability when they act 
as accessories to the offender's crime in the 
manner enumerated in Article 19 except for 
profiting or assisting the offender to profit by 
the effects of the crime. 
 
 
 
i. Fencers – P.D. No. 1612 
 
Fence is a person who commits the act of 
fencing. A fence who receives stolen 
property as above–provided is not an 
accessory but a principal in the crime defined 
by the law. 
 
Principal as a fence 
Under the Anti-Fencing Law (P.D. 1612), one 
who knowingly profits or assists the principal 
to profit from the effects of robbery or theft is 
NOT only an accessory but also principal 
for fencing. 
 


<!-- PAGE 27 -->

 
 
21 
 
ii. Obstructors of Criminal 
Prosecution – P.D. No. 1829 
 
Any person who knowingly or willfully 
obstructs, impedes, frustrates or delays 
the apprehension of suspects and the 
investigation and prosecution of criminal 
cases, shall be punished: 
 
1. Prision correccional in its 
maximum period, or 
2. Fine ranging from PhP 1,000 – 
6,000, or 
3. Both 
 
Separate charge 
 
An accused may or may not be an accessory 
to the crime but he may be charged 
separately for Obstruction of Justice under 
PD 1829. This law penalizes any person who 
knowingly or willfully obstructs, impedes, 
frustrates, or delays the apprehension of 
suspects 
and 
the 
investigation 
and 
prosecution of a person who committed a 
crime.

b. Conspiracy and Proposal 
 
Conspiracy. Exists when two or more 
persons come to an agreement concerning 
the commission of a felony and decide to 
commit it.  
 
GR: When conspiracy exists, the degree of 
participation of each conspirator is not 
considered because the act of one is the act 
of all; thus, they have equal criminal 
responsibility.  
 
XPN: Even though there was conspiracy, if a 
co-conspirator merely cooperated in the 
commission of the crime with insignificant or 
minimal acts, such that even without his 


<!-- PAGE 28 -->

 
 
22 
cooperation, the crime could be carried out 
as well, such co-conspirator should be 
punished as an accomplice only. (People v. 
Niem, CA No. 521, 20 Dec. 1945) 
 
XPN to the XPN: When the act constitutes a 
single indivisible offense. 
 
NOTE: Conspiracy must be proven beyond 
reasonable doubt by showing unity of 
purpose and intent to commit the crime. 
The acts cited against each accused largely 
reflect participation in implementation steps 
after the decision not to bid had been taken, 
as well as administrative or ministerial 
functions. Absent proof of undue injury 
and the requisite bad faith or gross 
negligence, there is no criminal design to 
which their acts can be tied. (★People. v. 
Lupoyon, 
et 
al., 
G.R. 
No. 
259467, 
November 11, 2024, as penned by 
J.Gaerlan) 
 
 
 
Requisites of Conspiracy 
 
1. Two or more persons came to an 
agreement;  
2. Agreement concerned the commission of 
a crime; and  
3. Execution of a felony was decided upon. 
 
Note: 
Mere 
knowledge, 
acquiescence to, or approval of the 
act, without cooperation or at least, 
agreement to cooperate, is not 
enough to constitute a conspiracy. 
Except when he is the mastermind in 
a conspiracy, it is necessary that a 
conspirator should have performed 
some overt act as a direct or indirect 
contribution in the execution of the 
crime planned to be committed. 
 
i. As a Mode of Incurring Criminal 
Liability 
When the conspiracy is only a basis of 
incurring criminal liability, there must be an 
overt act done before the co-conspirators 
become criminally liable. 
GR: If he appeared in the scene of the crime, 
he is liable as a co-conspirator.  
XPNs:  
1. If he is a mastermind, he does not have to 
be in the scene of the crime to be co-
conspirator.  
2. If he performs an overt act in the 
performance of the conspiracy, even if it is 
not in the scene of the crime per se (e.g., the 
driver of a get-away car who planned the 
crime as well, or the man who pressed the 
button of a remote-control bomb and the 
bomb exploded a few streets away). 
Ways in Committing Conspiracy 
1. Direct or Express Conspiracy - There is 
an express agreement.  
GR: The liability of the conspirators is only for 
the crime agreed upon.  
XPNs:  
a. The other crime was committed in their 
presence and they did not prevent its 
commission;  
b. When the other crime is the natural 
consequence of the crime planned (e.g., 
homicide resulting from physical injuries); or  
c. When the resulting crime was a composite 
crime or a special complex crime. 
2. Implied Conspiracy 
An implied conspiracy exists when two or 
more persons are shown to have aimed by 
their acts towards the accomplishment of the 
same unlawful object, each doing a part so 
that 
their 
combined 
acts, 
though 
apparently independent, were in fact 


<!-- PAGE 29 -->

 
 
23 
connected and cooperative, indicating 
closeness of personal association and a 
concurrence of sentiment. (People v. Evasco 
y Nugay, G.R. NO 213415, 2018). 
 
Implied conspiracy is proved through the 
mode and manner of the commission of the 
offense, or from the acts of the accused 
before, during and after the commission of 
the crime indubitably pointing to a joint 
purpose, a concert of action and a 
community of interest. (People v. Evasco y 
Nugay, G.R. NO 213415, 2018). 
Liability of conspirators 
GR: Act of one is the act of all. When the 
conspiracy 
is 
established, 
all 
who 
participated therein, regardless of their 
participation, are equally liable. 
Responsibility of a conspirator is not confined 
to the accomplishment of a particular 
purpose of conspiracy but extends to 
collateral acts and offenses incident to and 
growing out of the purpose intended.  
Thus, conspirators are still liable: 
i. 
When the other crime was committed 
in their presence and they did not prevent its 
commission which is taken as approval or 
acquiescence to the second crime;  
 ii.   When the other crime is the natural 
consequence of the crime planned e.g., 
homicide resulting from physical injuries 
 iii. When the resulting crime is a composite 
crime or special complex crime considered a 
single indivisible felony except when a 
conspirator is not aware of the commission of 
the second unplanned crime. 
 
 
XPN: When one or some of the conspirators 
committed another crime, which is not a part 
of the crime intended. 
Co-conspirators are criminally liable only for 
the acts done pursuant to the conspiracy on 
how and what are the necessary and logical 
consequences of the intended crime. 
 
Multiple Conspiracies 
 
a. The “wheel” or “circle” conspiracy, in 
which there is a single person or group (“the 
hub”) dealing individually with two or more 
other persons or groups (“the spokes”); and 
b. The “chain” conspiracy, usually involving 
the 
distribution 
of 
narcotics 
or 
other 
contraband, in which there is successive 
communication and cooperation in much the 
same way as with legitimate business 
operations 
between 
manufacturer 
and 
wholesaler, then wholesaler and retailer, and 
then retailer and consumer. (Estrada vs. 
Sandiganbayan, G.R. No. 148560 [2001]) 
 
 


<!-- PAGE 30 -->

 
 
24 
 
ii. As a Criminal Act 
 
The mere conspiracy is the crime itself. This 
is only true when the law expressly punishes 
the 
mere 
conspiracy. 
Otherwise, 
the 
conspiracy does not bring about the 
commission of the crime because conspiracy 
is not an overt act but a mere preparatory act.  
 
Conspiracy must be proven on the same 
quantum of evidence as the felony subject of 
the agreement of the parties. It may be 
proved by direct or circumstantial evidence 
consisting of acts, words, or conduct of the 
alleged conspirators prior to, during, and 
after the commission of the felony to achieve 
a common design or purpose. (Franco v. 
People, G.R. No. 171328, 16 Feb. 2011) 
 
 
 
Examples:  
 
1. Conspiracy to commit treason  
2. Conspiracy to commit rebellion  
3. Conspiracy to commit acts like 
sale, 
importation 
and 
distribution of drugs 
4. Conspiracy to commit access 
devise fraud 
5. Conspiracy 
to 
commit 
terrorism/rebellion 
 
 
Proposal 
 
Exists when the person who has decided to 
commit a felony proposes its execution to 
some other person or persons. 
 
Requisites of a Proposal  
 
1. A person has decided to commit a 
felony;  
2. He proposes its execution to other 
person or persons; and  
3. The proposal need not be 
accepted or else it shall be a 
conspiracy. 
Punishment for Conspiracy and Proposal to 
Commit a Felony  
GR: Conspiracy and proposal to commit a 
felony are not punishable because they are 
mere preparatory acts.  
XPN: When the law specifically provides a 
penalty thereof, i.e., treason, rebellion and 
coup d’etat 
Note: If there is conspiracy to commit 
rebellion, and rebellion is thereafter 
committed, the accused is liable only 
for rebellion, the conspiracy now 
being merely proof of the rebellion. 
 
Conspiracy vs. Proposal to Commit a 
Felony 
CONSPIRACY 
PROPOSAL 
As to its Existence 
It exists when two or 
more persons come 
to 
an 
agreement 
concerning 
the 
commission 
of 
a 
felony and decide to 
commit it. 
There 
is 
proposal 
when the person who 
has decided to commit 
a felony proposes its 
execution 
to 
some 
other 
person 
or 
persons. 
As to its Occurrence 
Once the proposal is 
accepted, 
a 
conspiracy arises. 
Proposal is true only 
up to the point where 
the party to whom the 
proposal was made 
has not yet accepted 
the proposal. 
As to the Number of Parties 
Conspiracy 
is 
bilateral. It requires 
two parties. 
Proposal is unilateral; 
one party makes a 
proposition 
to 
the 
other. 
 
7. Circumstances Affecting 
Criminal Liability 
 
a. Justifying Circumstances – RPC, Art. 
11 
 
Where the act of the person is said to be in 
accordance with the law, such person is 
deemed not to have transgressed the law. 


<!-- PAGE 31 -->

 
 
25 
 
Basis. Lack of dolo 
 
Criminal Liability. None; there is no crime 
committed, the act being justified. 
 
Civil Liability. None, except in par. 4, Art. 11 
(avoidance of greater evil), where the civil 
liability is borne by the persons benefited by 
the act in proportion to the benefit they may 
have received [Art. 101, RPC]. 
 
Burden of Proof. Rests on the accused 
[People v. Corecor, G.R. No. L-63155 
(1988)]. 
 
Admission by the accused. When a 
justifying circumstance is invoked, it implies 
the admission by the accused that he 
committed the criminal act. 
 
a. Defense of person, right, property, or 
honor (Self-Defense) 
Mnemonic 
Requisites 
 
1. Unlawful 
aggression; 
 
2. Reasonable 
necessity of means 
employed to prevent 
or repel it; and 
 
3. Lack of sufficient 
provocation on the 
part of the person 
defending himself. 
 
Why Self-Defense is Lawful 
1. Impulse of self-preservation; 
2. State cannot provide protection for each of 
its constituents (Castanares v. Court of 
Appeals, G.R. Nos. L-41269- 70 (1979). 
 
Accused makes an admission when 
invoking self-defense 
 
When the accused invokes self-defense, in 
effect, he or she admits to the commission of 
the acts for which he or she was charged, 
albeit under circumstances that, if proven, 
would exculpate him or her. 
 
Consequently, the burden of proving that the 
act was justified, shifts upon him or her. The 
accused must prove through clear and 
convincing evidence that there was URL. 
(★People v. Archivido, G.R. No. 233085, 
September 21, 2020, as penned by J. 
Gaerlan) 
 
i. Unlawful Aggression 
 
It presupposes Actual, Sudden/unexpected 
attack or IMminent (ASIm) danger thereof, 
not 
merely threatening 
or 
intimidating 
attitude. Note that the victim’s aggression, 
which prompted the accused to act in self-
defense, must be unlawful. 
 
There is an unlawful aggression when the 
peril to one’s life, limb, or right is either actual 
or imminent. 
 
Lawful Aggression 
 
When it is done: 
 
a. For the fulfillment of a duty 
b. In the exercise of a right in a more or 
less violent manner 
 
Self-defense is not retaliation 
 
When the killing of the deceased by the 
accused was after the attack made by the 
former, the accused must have no time nor 
occasion for deliberation and cool thinking. 


<!-- PAGE 32 -->

 
 
26 
When unlawful aggression ceases, the 
defender has no longer any right to kill or 
wound the former aggressor, otherwise, 
retaliation and not self-defense is committed. 
[People v. Bates, G.R. No. 139907 (2003)]. 
 
There is no unlawful aggression in the 
following circumstances: 
 
a. When there is an agreement to fight and 
the challenge to fight was accepted [People 
v. Navarro, G.R. No. L-1878 (1907)]. Note: 
the unlawful aggression must come from the 
victim. 
XPN: When the aggression which is 
ahead of an agreed time or place is 
unlawful aggression [Severino Justo 
v. Court of Appeals, G.R. No. L-8611 
(1956)]. 
 
b. When the paramour kills the offended 
husband who was assaulting him because 
the husband’s aggression was lawful [Art. 
247, RPC] [U.S. v. Merced, G.R. No. 14170 
(1918)]. 
 
c. Reasonable force made by a police officer 
to arrest a suspect [People v. Calip, et al., 3 
C.A. Rep. 808]. 
 
d. Mere belief in an impending attack is NOT 
sufficient. Neither is an intimidating or 
threatening attitude. (People vs. Bautista, 
G.R. No. 109800, March 12, 1996). 
 
BUT there is self-defense even if the 
aggressor used a toy pistol, provided the 
accused believed it was a real gun. (People 
vs. Boral, 11 CA 914) 
 
e. The act of the (deceased) of allegedly 
drawing a gun from his waist cannot be 
categorized as unlawful aggression. Such 
act did not put in real peril the life or personal 
safety of the appellant. 
 
f. The act of pulling “something” or pressing 
his right hand to his hip where a revolver was 
holstered or pulling a gun but without 
manifestation of any aggressive is not an 
unlawful aggression. Hence, killings of the 
victims are unjustified. But aiming a revolver 
at another with intent to shoot is an imminent 
unlawful 
aggression, 
within 
the 
contemplation of the justifying circumstance 
of self-defense. 
 
g. Thrusting hand into the pocket as if for the 
purpose of drawing a dagger or a pocket 
knife (U.S. vs. Carrero, G.R No. L-3956 
January 10, 1908) or pulling a kitchen knife 
(People vs. Escarlos, G.R. No. 148912, 
September 10, 2003) is not unlawful 
aggression; hence, the killing of the victim is 
not justified. However, opening a knife and 
making a motion as if to attack is an imminent 
unlawful aggression (People vs. Olarbe, G.R. 
No. 227421, July 23, 2018), which justified 
the exercise of self-defense. 
 
ii. Reasonable Necessity of the Means 
Employed to Prevent or Repeal the 
Unlawful Aggression 
 
There must be: 
 
(a) a necessity of the course of action 
taken by the person making a defense, 
and 
(b) a necessity of the means used. 
 
BOTH of which should be REASONABLE. 
 
 
Doctrine of Rational Equivalence 
 
Perfect equality between the weapon used 
by the one defending himself and that of the 
aggressor is NOT required, nor is the 
material commensurability between the 
means of attack and defense. Rational 
equivalence is enough [People v. Samson, 
G.R. No. 214883 (2015)] 


<!-- PAGE 33 -->

 
 
27 
 
Reason: Because the person assaulted 
does not have sufficient tranquility of mind to 
think and to calculate. 
 
Test of Reasonableness 
 
Reasonableness depends upon the: 
 
a. Nature and quality of the weapon used by 
the aggressor 
b. Aggressor’s physical condition, character, 
size, and other circumstances 
c. Circumstances of those of the person 
defending himself 
d. Place and occasion of the assault. [Escoto 
v. Court of Appeals, G.R. No. 118002 (1997)] 
 
When the Assault was Without Use of 
Weapon 
 
GR: A man is not justified in taking the life of 
one who assaults him with his fist only, 
without the use of a dangerous weapon. 
 
XPN: Where the person assaulted has 
retreated to the wall and uses in a defensive 
way the only weapon at his disposal [People 
v. Sumicad, G.R. No. 35524 (1932)]. 
 
Rules in the Use of Firearms 
 
a. If the attacker is already disarmed - there 
is no need to further use violence [People v. 
Masangkay, G.R. No. L- 73461 (1988)]. 
 
b. If the attacker was disarmed but struggled 
to re-obtain the weapon - violence may be 
justified [People v. Rabandaban, G.R. No. L-
2228 (1950)]. 
 
c. The one defending must aim at the 
defendant and not indiscriminately fire his 
deadly weapon [People v. Galacgac, C.A., 
54 O.G. 1027]. 
 
iii. Lack of Sufficient Provocation on the 
Part of the Person Defending 
 
The accused who claims self-defense must 
not have provoked the aggression of the 
victim. 
 
Provocation 
is 
sufficient 
when 
it 
is 
proportionate to the aggression 
 
Defense of Property 
 
The defense of property rights can be 
invoked if there is an attack upon the 
property, although it is not coupled with an 
attack upon the person of the owner of the 
premises, so long as all the elements for 
justification must 
however 
be 
present 
[People v. Narvaez, G.R. No. L-33466- 67 
(1983)]. 
 
Defense of Home 
 
Violent entry to another’s house at nighttime; 
by a person who is armed with a bolo; and 
forcing his way into the house, shows he was 
ready and looking for trouble [People v. 
Mirabiles, 45 O.G., 5th Supp., 277]. 
 
Defense of Honor and Reputation 
 
Placing a hand by a man on the woman’s 
upper thigh is unlawful aggression [People v. 
Jaurigue, C.A. No. 384 (1946)]. 
 
A slap on the face is also considered as 
unlawful 
aggression 
since 
the 
face 
represents a person and his dignity [Rugas 
v. People, G.R. No. 147789 (2004)]. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 


<!-- PAGE 34 -->

 
 
28 
b. Defense of relatives 
Mnemonic 
Requisites 
 
 
1. 
Unlawful 
aggression; 
 
2. 
Reasonable 
necessity of means 
employed to prevent 
or repel it; and 
 
3. Lack of sufficient 
provocation on part of 
relative, or, in case of 
provocation, the one 
making the defense 
had No part therein. 
 
 
Relatives Covered by Par. 2 [SAD-SiRC] 
 
1.   Spouse 
2.   Ascendants 
3.   Descendants 
4.  Legitimate, natural, or adopted Siblings, 
or relatives by affinity in the same degrees 
(parents-in-laws, hildren-in-law, siblings-
in-law) 
5. Relatives by Consanguinity within 4th civil 
degree 
 
Note: Death of one spouse does not 
terminate the relationship by affinity 
established between the surviving 
spouse and the blood relatives of the 
deceased. 
(Intestate 
Estate 
of 
Manolita 
Gonzales 
Vda 
De 
Carungcong vs. People, G.R. No. 
181409, February 11, 2010) 
 
 
 
 
Existence 
of 
danger 
(Unlawful 
Aggression) 
 
Unlawful aggression can be made to depend 
on the honest belief of the one making a 
defense. However, one may no longer be 
exempt from criminal liability when he or the 
person being defended is no longer in danger 
of bodily harm by the aggressor [US v. 
Esmedia, G.R. No. L-5740 (1910)]. 
 
No sufficient provocation from defender 
 
Even if the relative being defended is the 
unlawful aggressor, an honest belief of the 
one making the defense that such was not 
the fact will be justified, provided all other 
requisites are present. 
 
c. Defense of strangers 
Stranger is any person not included in the 
enumeration of relatives under par. 2 of Art. 
11. 
 
 
Mnemonic 
Requisites 
 
1. Unlawful aggression; 
 
2. Reasonable necessity 
of the means employed 
to prevent or repel it; and 
 
3. The person defending 
was not Induced by 
revenge, resentment or 
other evil motive 
 
Note: Motive is relevant in this kind of 
defense. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 


<!-- PAGE 35 -->

 
 
29 
d. 
Avoidance of greater evil 
Mnemonic 
Requisites 
 
1. Evil sought to be 
avoided 
actually 
Exists. 
 
2. Evil or injury must 
Not 
have 
been 
produced by the one 
invoking 
the 
justifying 
circumstances. 
 
3. Injury feared be 
greater 
than 
that 
done to avoid it; and 
 
4. There are no other 
practical and less 
harmful Means of 
preventing it. 
 
State of Necessity - There is necessity 
when the situation is of grave peril, actual or 
imminent. It is indispensable that the state of 
necessity is not brought about by intentional 
provocation on the part of the party invoking 
the same (People v. Retubado, G.R. No. 
124058 (2003). 
 
In cases falling within the subdivision 4 of Art. 
11, the persons who benefited from the 
doctrine of state necessity, shall be civilly 
liable in proportion to the benefit, which they 
may have received. 
 
Kind of Evil - The evil contemplated includes 
injury to persons and damage to property. 
This greater evil must not be brought about 
by the negligence or imprudence of the 
accused 
 
 
 
 
 
e. Fulfillment of duty or lawful exercise 
of right 
 
Mnemonic 
Requisites 
 
1. Offender acted in 
Performance of duty or 
in the lawful exercise 
of a right or office; and 
 
2. The injury caused or 
the offense committed 
be 
the 
Necessary 
consequence of the 
due performance of 
duty 
or 
the 
lawful 
exercise of such right 
or office. 
 
Police Officers 
 
The law does not clothe police officers with 
authority to arbitrarily judge the necessity to 
kill, but must be within reasonable limits. 
 
A police officer is not required to afford the 
victim the opportunity to fight back. Neither is 
he expected, when hard pressed and in the 
heat of such an encounter at close quarters, 
to pause for a long moment and reflect coolly 
at his peril, or to wait after each blow to 
determine the effects thereof. But he cannot 
be exonerated from overdoing his duty when 
he fatally shot the victim in the head, even 
after the latter slumped to the ground due to 
multiple gunshot wounds sustained while 
charging at the police officers. Sound 
discretion and restraint dictated that a 
veteran policeman should have ceased firing 
at the victim the moment he saw the latter fall 
to the ground. The victim at that point no 
longer posed a threat. Shooting him in the 
head was obviously unnecessary. (People 
vs. Ulep, G.R. No. 132547, September 20, 
2000) 
 
 
 


<!-- PAGE 36 -->

 
 
30 
Warning Shot 
 
To justify the killing by a policeman of a 
suspected robber as fulfillment of his lawful 
duty, he must have first fire a warning shot 
before shooting. 
 
However, the duty to issue a warning is not 
absolutely mandated at all times and at all 
cost, to the detriment of the life of law 
enforcers. The directive to issue a warning 
contemplates a situation where several 
options are available to the officers. When 
the threat to the life of the law enforcer was 
imminent and there was no other option but 
to use force, the law enforcer’s failure to 
issue a warning is excusable.  
 
 
f. Obedience to Superior Order (OPM) 
Obedience to a lawful order of a superior 
 
Art. 11(6) of the RPC, presupposes that what 
was obeyed by the accused was a lawful 
order; but if the accused complied with an 
unlawful order under a mistake of fact, he 
should not incur criminal liability. Thus, the 
subordinate is not liable for carrying out an 
illegal order if he is not aware of its illegality 
and he is not negligent. (Tabuena vs. 
Sandiganbayan, G.R. No. 103501–03) 
 
The application of the law is not limited to 
orders made by public officers to inferior 
public officials. Thus, a driver of an escaping 
prisoner who did not know that his employer 
is leaving the prison compound, as he used 
to drive for him to go to his office in previous 
incidents in order to escape, cannot be held 
criminally liable. 
 
One who prepared a falsified document with 
full knowledge of its falsity is not excused 
even if he merely acted in obedience to the 
instruction of his superior, because the 
instruction was not for a lawful purpose 
(People. v. Barroga, G.R. No. L-31563) 
 
A soldier who tortured to death the deceased 
in obedience to the order of his sergeant is 
not exempt from liability because the order to 
torture the deceased was illegal, and the 
accused was not bound to obey it [People. v. 
Margen, et al., G.R. No. L-2681 (1950)]. 
 
 
b. Exempting Circumstances – RPC, Art. 
12 
The basic reason behind the enactment of 
exempting circumstances is the complete 
absence of intelligence, freedom of action, or 
intent, or on the absence of negligence on 
the part of the accused. (Ortega v. People, 
G.R. No. 151085, August 20,2008) 
i. Minority – R.A. No. 9344, Sec. 6, as 
amended 
 
Child/Minor - refers to a person under the 
age of eighteen (18) years. 
 
Child in Conflict with the Law (CICL) - 
refers to a child who is alleged as, accused 
of, or adjudged as, having committed an 
offense under Philippine laws. 
 
Discernment - is the mental capacity of the 
child at the time of the commission of the 
offense to understand the differences 
between 
right 
and 
wrong 
and 
the 
consequences of the wrongful act. 
 
Intervention - a series of activities designed 
to address issues that caused the child to 
Mnemonic 
Requisites 
 
1. Order must have 
been 
issued 
by 
a 
superior; 
2. The order is for some 
lawful Purpose; and 
3. Means used to carry 
it out must be lawful. 


<!-- PAGE 37 -->

 
 
31 
commit an offense. It may take the form of an 
individualized treatment program which may 
include counseling, skills training, education, 
and other activities that will enhance his/her 
psychological, emotional, and psycho-social 
well-being 
 
 
Basis of exemption from criminal liability 
 
The law presumes that the minor offenders 
completely lack the intelligence to distinguish 
right from wrong, so that their acts are 
deemed involuntary ones for which they 
cannot be held accountable. 
 
Note: Exemption only applies to 
criminal liability, and it does not 
extend to civil liability. 
 
Presumption of minority 
 
A child in conflict with the law enjoys the 
presumption of minority. [Sec. 5, A.M. No. 
01- 1-18-SC]. In case of doubt as to the age 
of the child, it shall be resolved in his/her 
favor. [Sec. 7, R.A. 9344] 
 
Determination of Age 
 
A child’s age can be proven by the following: 
 
1. Child’s birth certificate 
2. Baptismal certificate 
3. Other pertinent documents 
4. In the absence of such documents, age 
may be based on: 
 
a. Testimony of the child 
b. Testimony of a member of the family 
related by affinity or consanguinity 
c. Testimony of other persons 
d. Physical appearance of the child 
e. Other relevant evidence [Sec. 5. A.M. 
No. 01-1-18-SC] 
 
Any person alleging the age of the child in 
conflict with the law has the burden of 
proving the age of such child. [Sec. 7, R.A. 
9344]
