f. Absolutory Causes 
 
Those where the act committed is a crime but 
for reasons of public policy and sentiment 
there is no penalty imposed (People v. 
Talisic, G.R. No. 97961 (1997). 
 
Absolutory 
causes 
are 
exempting 
circumstances outside Art. 12, such as 
exempted accessories (Relatives who are 
exempt from criminal liability in cases of theft, 
estafa, or malicious mischief.) 
 
 
 
Types of Absolutory Causes 
 
1. Instigation 
2. Certain accessories in Art. 20. 
3. Accessories in light felonies (Art. 16) 
4. Physical injuries inflicted under 
exceptional circumstances (Art. 247) 
5. Spontaneous desistance in attempted 
felony (Art. 6) 
 
g. Instigation and Entrapment 
Instigation It is the means by which the 
accused is lured into the commission of the 
offense charged in order to prosecute him 
(People v. Bartolome, G.R. No 191726, 
2013).  
Entrapment v. Instigation  
There is entrapment when law officers 
employ ruses and schemes to ensure the 
apprehension of the criminal while in the 
actual commission of the crime. There is 
instigation when the accused was induced to 
commit the crime.  
The difference in the nature of the two lies in 
the 
origin 
of 
the 
criminal 
intent. 
In 
entrapment, the mens rea originates from 
the mind of the criminal. The idea and the 
resolve to commit the crime comes from him. 
In instigation, the law officer conceives the 
commission of the crime and suggests to the 
accused who adopts the idea and carries it 
into execution. 
Restitution/Return is not absolutory  
If the felony is consummated, the offender 
cannot undo what was done. Offender would 
not be absolved from criminal liability even if 


<!-- PAGE 71 -->

 
 
65 
he had done something that will mitigate the 
effects of the felonious act. 
Examples:  
(1) 
Restitution 
of 
funds 
malversed 
immediately and voluntarily made before the 
case was instituted is not an absolutory 
cause (Navarro vs. Meneses III, CBD Adm. 
Case No. 313, January 30, 1998) 
 
(2) “A” stole chicken under the house of “B” 
one evening. Realizing that what he did was 
wrong, “A” returned the chicken to the place 
under the house of “B”. Since the crime of 
theft was already consummated, the return of 
the stolen property does not relieve “A” of 
criminal responsibility. “A” had already 
performed all the acts of execution, which 
produced the crime of theft before he 
returned the chicken (Reyes). 
 
(3) The fact that the accused abandoned 
victim after six days of captivity does not 
lessen his criminal culpability much less 
exempt him from criminal liability for the 
kidnapping and detention of the victim 
(Baldogo, G.R. No. 128106-07, January 24, 
2003). 
 
 
8. Effect of Repeat Commission of 
Crimes 
 
a. Recidivism – RPC, Art. 14(9) 
 
A recidivist is one who, at the time of his trial 
for one crime, shall have been previously 
convicted by final judgment of another crime 
embraced in the same title of the revised 
penal code.  
 
b. Quasi-recidivism – RPC, Art. 160 
 
Any person who shall commit a felony after 
having been convicted by final judgment, 
before beginning to serve such sentence, or 
while serving the same, shall be punished by 
the 
maximum 
period 
of 
the 
penalty 
prescribed by law for the new felony. 
 
Any convict of the class referred to in this 
article, who is not a habitual criminal, shall be 
pardoned at the age of seventy years if he 
shall have already served out his original 
sentence, or when he shall complete it after 
reaching said age, unless by reason of his 
conduct or other circumstances he shall not 
be worthy of such clemency. 
 
c. Habituality – RPC, Art. 14(10) 
 
That the offender has been previously 
punished for an offense to which the law 
attaches an equal or greater penalty or for 
two or more crimes to which it attaches a 
lighter penalty. 
 
d. Habitual Delinquency – RPC, Art. 
62(5) 
A person shall be deemed to be habitual 
delinquent, if within a period of ten years from 
the date of his release or last conviction of 
the 
crimes 
of robo, hurto, estafa, 
or falsification, he is found guilty of any of 
said crimes a third time or oftener. 


<!-- PAGE 72 -->

 
 
66 
Habitual delinquency shall have the following 
effects: 
Upon a third conviction the culprit shall be 
sentenced to the penalty provided by law for 
the last crime of which he be found guilty and 
to 
the 
additional 
penalty 
of prision 
correccional in its medium and maximum 
periods; 
Upon a fourth conviction the culprit shall be 
sentenced to the penalty provided for the last 
crime of which he be found guilty and to the 
additional penalty of prision mayor in its 
minimum and medium periods; and 
Upon a fifth or additional conviction, the 
culprit shall be sentenced to the penalty 
provided for the last crime of which he be 
found guilty and to the additional penalty 
of prision mayor in its maximum period 
to reclusion temporal in its minimum period. 
Note: The total of the two penalties to be 
imposed upon the offender, in conformity 
herewith, shall in no case exceed 30 years.

9. Criminal Liability of Minors – R.A. 
No. 9344, as amended 
 
Governing Policy and Construction 
R.A. No. 9344 establishes a comprehensive 
juvenile justice and welfare system grounded 
on the best interest of the child, restorative 
justice, 
and 
rehabilitation 
rather 
than 
retribution. The law shall be liberally 
construed in favor of the child in conflict with 
the 
law 
(CICL). 
In 
all 
proceedings, 
deprivation of liberty is a measure of last 
resort and for the shortest appropriate 
period. 
CICL 
A Child in Conflict with the Law (CICL) refers 
to a person below eighteen (18) years of age 
who is alleged as, accused of, or adjudged 
as having committed an offense under 
Philippine laws, whether under the Revised 
Penal Code or special laws. 
Minimum Age of Responsibility 
A. Children Fifteen (15) Years Old or 
Below 
A child fifteen (15) years old or below at the 
time of the commission of the offense is 
exempt from criminal liability. However, such 
child is not exempt from intervention, and 
shall be subjected to an intervention program 
determined by the local social welfare and 
development officer. 
 
 
 
B. Children Above Fifteen (15) But Below 
Eighteen (18) Years Old 
A child above fifteen (15) but below eighteen 
(18) years of age is likewise exempt from 
criminal liability, UNLESS he or she acted 
with discernment. 
 
Discernment refers to the mental capacity of 
the child to understand the wrongfulness of 
the 
act 
and 
its 
consequences. 
The 
determination of discernment is a factual 
matter, based on the child’s conduct before, 
during, and after the commission of the 
offense, and is assessed during the 
proceedings. 
If the child acted without discernment, he 
or she shall be subjected to an intervention 
program. 
If the child acted with discernment, he or 
she shall be subjected to appropriate 
proceedings under R.A. No. 9344, including 
possible diversion or court proceedings. 
The exemption from criminal liability does not 
include exemption from civil liability, which 
shall be enforced in accordance with existing 
laws. 
Determination of Age 
A child enjoys the presumption of minority. 
In case of doubt as to age, such doubt shall 
be resolved in favor of the child. 
Age may be determined through: 
 Birth certificate 
 
 Baptismal certificate 
 
 Other relevant documents 


<!-- PAGE 75 -->

 
 
67 
 
 In their absence, testimonies, 
physical appearance, and 
other evidence 
Pending determination of age, proceedings 
in the main case shall be suspended. 
 
Diversion 
Diversion refers to an alternative, child-
appropriate 
process 
of 
determining 
responsibility and treatment without resorting 
to formal court proceedings. 
Diversion may be conducted at: 
 Katarungang Pambarangay level 
 
 Police investigation 
 
 Inquest or preliminary investigation 
Judicial level 
As a rule, diversion is mandatory where the 
imposable penalty is not more than six (6) 
years, subject to statutory conditions. Where 
the imposable penalty exceeds six (6) years, 
diversion may be resorted to only by the 
court. 
Suspension of Sentence 
A child who is found guilty of the offense 
charged shall be entitled to automatic 
suspension of sentence, without need of 
application, provided that the child was below 
eighteen (18) years of age at the time of the 
commission of the offense, even if already 
above eighteen (18) at the time of judgment. 
Instead 
of 
pronouncing 
judgment 
of 
conviction, 
the 
court 
shall 
impose 
appropriate disposition measures under the 
Supreme Court Rule on Juveniles in Conflict 
with the Law. 
Prohibition on Life Imprisonment and 
Death Penalty 
No child in conflict with the law shall be: 
 Sentenced to death penalty; or 
 
 Sentenced to life imprisonment 
without 
the 
possibility 
of 
release 
C. PENALTIES 
 
1.Classification 
 
a. Major Classification 
 
1. Principal Penalties - Those 
expressly imposed by the court in the 
judgment of conviction. 
 
2. Accessory Penalties - Those that 
are 
deemed 
included 
in 
the 
imposition of the principal penalties. 
 
3. Subsidiary Penalties - Those 
imposed in lieu of principal penalties, 
i.e., imprisonment in case of inability 
to pay the fine 
 
b. According to Divisibility 
 
1. Divisible - Those that have fixed duration 
and can be divided into three periods: 
minimum, medium, maximum. 
 
a. Reclusion temporal 
b. Prision mayor 
c. Prision correccional 
d. Suspension 
e. Destierro 
f. Arresto mayor 
g. Arresto menor 
 
2. Indivisible - Those that have no fixed 
duration. 
 
a. Death 
b. Reclusion perpetua 
c. Perpetual absolute/special 
disqualification 
d. Public censure 
 


<!-- PAGE 76 -->

 
 
68 
 
c. According to Subject-Matter 
 
1. Corporal - Death. 
2. Deprivation of freedom - 
Reclusion, prision, arresto. 
3. Restriction of freedom - Destierro. 
4. Deprivation of Rights - 
Disqualification, suspension. 
5. Pecuniary - Fine. 
 
d. According to Gravity 
 
1. Capital 
2. Afflictive 
3. Correctional 
4. Light 
 
Three scales of penalties in the Code 
and their significance: 
 
i. Art. 25 classifies the penalties into 
principal and accessories; 
 
ii. Art. 70 provides for the scale when 
there are two or more sentences to 
be served, including the three–fold 
rule; 
 
iii. Art. 71 graduates the penalties into 
the order of severity for purposes of 
applying the rules under Art. 61 in 
relation to Art. 50–57, the penalty 
imposable on principal, accomplices, 
and accessories for consummated, 
frustrated, and attempted felonies. 
 
2. PRINCIPAL AND ACCESSORY 
PENALTIES  
 
A. Principal Penalties: Capital Punishment 
(Death) 
 
B. Afflictive Penalties 
 
1. Reclusion perpetua 
2. Reclusion temporal 
3. Perpetual or temporary absolute 
disqualification 
4. Perpetual or temporary special 
disqualification 
5. Prision mayor 
 
 
C. Correctional penalties  
 
1. Prision correccional 
2. Arresto mayor 
3. Suspension 
4. Destierro 
 
 
D. Light penalties  
 
1. Arresto menor 
2. Public censure 
 
E. Penalties Common to the Three 
Preceding Classes (FB) 
 
a. Fine; and  
b. Bond to Keep the Peace. 
 
Scale of Accessory Penalties 
 
1. Perpetual or Temporary Absolute 
Disqualification;  
 
2. Perpetual or Temporary Special 
DisqualiFication;  
 
3. Suspension from public office, the 
right to vote and be voted for, the 
profession or calling; 
 
4. Civil interdiction;  
 
5. Indemnification;  
 
6. Forfeiture or confiscation of 
instruments and proceeds of the 
offense; and  
 
7. Payment of Costs

CAPITAL PUNISHMENT 
 
Death (The imposition of death penalty is 
now prohibited under R.A. No. 9346.) 
 
Accessory Penalties of Death 
Death, when not executed by reason of 
commutation or pardon, shall carry with it:  
1. Perpetual Absolute DisqualiFication  
2. Civil Interdiction during the First 30 years 
following the date of the sentence. (Art. 40, 
RPC)  
Such 
accessory 
penalties 
shall 
be 
continuously suffered by the convict even if 
the principal penalty has been pardoned; 
unless such penalties have been expressly 
remitted in the pardon. 
In lieu of death penalty, following shall be 
imposed:  
a. Reclusion perpetua (If the law 
violated uses the RPC’s nomenclature) 
b. Life imprisonment - (If it does not, 
Sec. 2, R.A. 9346) 
Reclusion Perpetua vs. Life Imprisonment 
Reclusion 
perpetua 
Life imprisonment 
As to Applicability 
Pertains 
to 
the 
penalty imposed for 
violation of the RPC 
Pertains to the penalty 
imposed for violation of 
special laws 
As to Duration 
It has Fixed duration It has no Fixed duration 
As to Accessory Penalties 
It carries with it 
accessory penalties 
It does not carry with it 
accessory penalty 
 
The death penalty, when it is not executed by 
reason of commutation or pardon shall carry with 
it that of perpetual absolute dis qualification and 
that of civil interdiction during 30 years following 
the date of sentence, unless such accessory 
penal ties have been expressly remitted in the 
pardon. (Art. 40, RPC) 
 
AFFLICTIVE PENALTIES 
 
A. Reclusion perpetua 
 
In R.A. 7659, the penalty of reclusion perpetua is 
now accorded a defined duration ranging from 
twenty (20) years and one (1) day to forty (40) 
years. 
 
Reclusion perpetua remains to be an indivisible 
penalty and, when it is the prescribed penalty, 
should be imposed in its entirety, i.e., reclusion 


<!-- PAGE 78 -->

 
 
70 
perpetua sans a fixed period for its duration, 
regardless of any mitigating or aggravating 
circumstance that may have attended the 
commission of the crime [People v. Ticalo, G.R. 
No. 138990 (2002)]. 
 
B. Reclusion Temporal 
 
Reclusion temporal is the most severe of the 
divisible penalties with duration of 12 years and 
1 day to 20 years.  
 
Only one day separates the divisible penalty of 
reclusion temporal from the indivisible penalty of 
reclusion perpetua.  
The penalties of reclusion perpetua and 
reclusion temporal shall carry with them the 
accessory penalties of civil interdiction for life or 
during the period of the sentence as the case 
may be, and that of perpetual absolute 
disqualification which the offender shall suffer 
even though pardoned as to the principal 
penalty, unless the same shall have been 
expressly remitted in the pardon. (Art. 41, RPC) 
 
C. Prision mayor 
 
A penalty of imprisonment the duration of which 
is from six years and one day to twelve years. 
(Art. 27, RPC) 
 
The penalty of prision mayor shall carry with it 
that of temporary absolute disqualification and 
that of perpetual special disqualification from the 
right of suffrage which the offender shall suffer 
although pardoned as to the principal penalty, 
unless the same shall have been expressly 
remitted in the pardon. (Art. 42, RPC) 
 
CORRECTIONAL PENALTIES 
 
A. Prision correccional 
 
A penalty of imprisonment the duration of which 
is from six months and one day to six years.  
 
The penalty of prision correccional shall carry 
with it that of suspension from public office, from 
the right to follow a profession or calling, and that 
of perpetual special disqualification from the right 
of suffrage, if the duration of said imprisonment 
shall exceed eighteen months. The offender 
shall suffer the disqualification provided in the 
article although pardoned as to the principal 
penalty, unless the same shall have been 
expressly remitted in the pardon. (Art. 43, RPC)  
 
B. Arresto mayor 
 
The duration of the penalty shall be from one 
month and one day to 6 months. (Art. 27, as 
amended by RA 7659) 
 
Accessory Penalties of Arresto Mayor  
1. Suspension of the right to hold public 
office; and  
2. Suspension of the right of suffrage 
during the term of the sentence. (Art. 44, 
RPC) 
 
Suspension  
1. If imposed as a principal penalty: 6 months 
and 1 day to 6 years  


<!-- PAGE 79 -->

 
 
71 
2. If imposed as an accessory penalty: its 
duration shall be that of the principal penalty (Art. 
27, RPC) 
 
Effects of Suspension Suspension from:  
1. Public ofFice;  
2. Profession or calling; and  
3. Right of suffrage. (Art. 33, RPC)  
 
C. Destierro 
Destierro also lasts from 6 months and 1 day to 
6 years (Art. 27, RPC).  
Destierro is a principal penalty. It is a punishment 
whereby a convict is banished to a certain place 
and is prohibited from entering or coming near 
that place designated in the sentence, which 
shall be not more than 250 and not less than 25 
kilometers from the place designated. (Art. 87, 
RPC)  
If the convict should enter the prohibited places, 
he will be committing the crime of evasion of 
service of sentence under Art. 157. 
Although 
destierro 
does 
not 
constitute 
imprisonment, it is still a deprivation of liberty. 
Art. 29 of the RPC applies when the penalty is 
destierro.  
Destierro applies in the following cases: 
 (F- ICER) 
a. In case of Failure to give bond for good 
behavior (Art. 284, RPC) 
b. Serious physical Injuries 
c. Penalty of Concubine in concubinage 
(Art. 334, RPC) 
d. Death under Exceptional circumstance 
(Art. 247, RPC) 
e. In cases where after Reducing the 
penalty by one or more degrees, destierro is 
the proper penalty. 
Note: The RPC does not provide for any 
accessory penalty for destierro. 
 
 
 
LIGHT PENALTIES 
 
A. Arresto Menor 
 
The duration of the penalty shall be one day to 
30 days. (Art. 27, as amended by RA 7659).  
 
The penalty shall be served in the municipal jail, 
or in the house of the defendant himself under 
the surveillance of an officer of the law, when the 
court so provided in its decision, taking into 
consideration the health of the offender and 
other reasons which may seem satisfactory to it. 
(Art 88, RPC) 
The penalty of “arresto” shall carry with it that of 
suspension of the right to hold office and the right 
of suffrage during the term of the sentence. 
 
B. Public Censure 
 
Public censure is a light penalty and is 
considered under the graduated scale provided 
in Article 71 as a penalty lower than arresto 
menor. It follows that the offense of reckless 
imprudence resulting in slight physical injuries 
for which it is prescribed is a light felony. 
(Reodica v. CA, G.R. No. 125066, July 8, 1998) 
 
C. Bond to keep the peace 
 
Bond to keep the peace is a principal penalty 
yet there is no crime in Book II for which it 
can be imposed. Therefore, following Article 
21, there is no occasion to impose this 
penalty.

72 
4. Application and Graduation and  
5. DETERMINATION OF IMPOSABLE 
PENALTY (Note: The Topics “4. Application 
for Graduation” and “5. Determination of 
Penalty” are combined IN THIS SECTION) 
 
 
Rules for the computation of penalties 
 
The following rules must be observed by the 
Director of Prisons or the warden in 
computing the penalties imposed upon 
convicts: 
 
1. When the offender is in prison 
 
The duration of temporary penalties is 
counted from the day on which the judgment 
of conviction becomes final. 
 
Ratio: The duration of temporary penalties 
shall be computed only from the date the 
judgment of conviction becomes final, and 
not from the day of detention, because under 
Article 24 of the RPC, arrest and temporary 
detention are not considered penalties. 
 
2. When the offender is not in prison 
 
The duration of a penalty consisting in 
deprivation of liberty is counted from the day 
the offender is placed at the disposal of 
judicial authorities for the enforcement of the 
penalty. 
 
3. Duration of other penalties 
 
The duration is counted from the day on 
which the offender commences to serve his 
sentence. 
 
Examples (temporary penalties) 
 
1. Temporary absolute disqualification 
2. Temporary special disqualification 
3. Suspension 
 
 
 
 
Applicability of the rules in cases of 
temporary penalties when the offender is 
not under detention because he was 
released on bail 
 
The duration is counted from the day on 
which the offender commences to serve his 
sentence. 
 
Examples 
(penalties 
consisting 
in 
deprivation of liberty) 
 
1. Imprisonment 
2. Destierro 
 
Factors to be considered in computing 
the proper imposable penalty 
 
1. Prescribed or graduated penalty 
2. Whether the penalty is indivisible or 
divisible 
3. Applicability or non-applicability of 
the Indeterminate Sentence Law 
(ISLAW) 
 
Prescribed penalty 
 
The prescribed penalty is the penalty 
provided in Book II of the Revised Penal 
Code. 
 
Graduated penalty 
 
The graduated penalty is the imposable 
penalty after considering the applicable 
graduating factors. 
 
Graduating factors 
 
1. Stages of execution 
2. Nature of participation 
3. Presence of privileged mitigating 
circumstance 
 
Privileged 
mitigating 
circumstances 
under the Revised Penal Code 
 
1. When the offender is a minor under 
eighteen (18) years of age 
(Article 68, RPC) 
 


<!-- PAGE 81 -->

 
 
73 
2. When the crime committed is not 
wholly excusable 
(Article 69, RPC) 
 
3. When there are two or more 
mitigating circumstances and no 
aggravating circumstance, the court 
shall impose the penalty next lower 
to that prescribed by law, in the 
period it may deem applicable 
according to the number and nature 
of such circumstances 
(Article 64[5], RPC) 
 
4. Voluntary release of the person 
illegally detained within three (3) 
days, without the offender attaining 
his purpose and before the 
institution of the criminal action 
(Article 268[3], RPC) 
 
5. Abandonment without justification by 
the offended spouse in case of 
adultery 
(Article 333[3], RPC) 
 
6. Concealing dishonor in case of 
infanticide 
(Article 255[2], RPC) 
 
Privileged 
mitigating 
circumstances 
contemplated under Article 69 of the 
Revised Penal Code 
 
1. Incomplete justifying circumstances 
(Article 11, RPC) 
2. Incomplete exempting circumstances 
(Article 12, RPC) 
 
Provided that the majority of their requisites 
are present. 
 
Requisites for the application of Article 69 
of the Revised Penal Code 
 
1. Some of the conditions required to 
justify the act or exempt from 
criminal liability are lacking; 
2. The majority of such conditions are 
nevertheless present; 
Note: If there are only two requisites, 
the presence of one constitutes 
majority; and 
3. When the circumstance has an 
indispensable element, such 
element must be present. 
(Regalado, 2007) 
 
Instances 
when 
mitigating 
and 
aggravating 
circumstances 
are 
not 
considered in the imposition of penalty 
 
1. When the penalty is single and 
indivisible 
2. In 
felonies 
committed 
through 
negligence 
3. When the penalty to be imposed 
upon a Moro or other non-Christian 
inhabitants lies within the discretion 
of the trial court, regardless of 
attending circumstances 
4. When the penalty imposed is only a 
fine under an ordinance 
5. When the penalties are prescribed by 
special laws 
 
Rules for the application of divisible 
penalties (Article 64, RPC) 
 
Divisible penalties include: 
 
1. Penalty composed of three (3) 
periods 
2. Penalty not composed of three (3) 
periods 
3. Complex penalty 
4. Penalty without a specific legal form 
(Campanilla, 2017) 
 
Penalty composed of three (3) periods 
 
When the penalty is composed of three (3) 
periods, the rules under Article 64 of the 
Revised Penal Code shall apply. 
 
If the penalty is composed of three (3) 
periods 
1. No 
mitigating 
and 
aggravating 
circumstance 
 
The penalty shall be imposed in the 
medium period. 


<!-- PAGE 82 -->

 
 
74 
 
2. One (1) mitigating circumstance 
The penalty shall be imposed in the 
minimum period. 
 
3. One (1) aggravating circumstance 
The penalty shall be imposed in the 
maximum period. 
 
4. When there are both mitigating and 
aggravating 
circumstances 
The court shall offset the mitigating 
circumstances 
against 
the 
aggravating circumstances according 
to their relative weight. 
 
5. Two 
(2) 
or 
more 
mitigating 
circumstances and no aggravating 
circumstance. 
 
The penalty shall be imposed one 
degree lower, in the period that the 
court may deem applicable according 
to the number and nature of such 
mitigating circumstances. 
 
6. Limitation on the imposition of penalty 
 
No penalty greater than the maximum 
period of the penalty prescribed by 
law shall be imposed, regardless of 
the 
number 
of 
aggravating 
circumstances present.

6. Preventive Imprisonment 
 
Preventive imprisonment refers to the 
detention of the accused while the case 
against him is ongoing trial either because: 
a) the crime he committed is a non-bailable 
offense and evidence of guilt is strong; b) or 
the crime is committed a bailable offense but 
he does not have the capacity to post bail. 
 
Rules 
 
1. If detention prisoner voluntarily agrees in 
writing, after being informed of the effects 
thereof with the assistance of counsel, to 
abide by the same disciplinary rules imposed 
upon convicted prisoners 
 
GR: Period of preventive imprisonment shall 
be fully credited in the service of the 
sentence 
 
 
 
 


<!-- PAGE 85 -->

 
 
77 
XPNs: 
a) recidivists, or have been convicted 
previously twice or more times of any 
crime 
b) those who failed to surrender 
voluntarily, 
after 
having 
been 
summoned for the execution of the 
sentence 
 
2. If the detention prisoner does not agree, 
the deduction shall be 4/5 of the time during 
which 
he 
has 
undergone 
preventive 
imprisonment. (People v. Abanes, 73 SCRA 
44) 
 
Application of Preventive Imprisonment 
to Reclusion Perpetua or Destierro 
  
Article 29 on the deduction of the period of 
preventive imprisonment applies where the 
accused is sentenced to destierro because 
destierro also constitutes deprivation of 
liberty. (Bastasa, February 1979). 
 
 
7. Subsidiary Penalty 
 
It is imposed upon the accused and served 
by him in lieu of the fine which he fails to pay 
on account of insolvency at the rate of one 
day for each amount equivalent to the 
highest minimum wage rate prevailing in the 
Philippines at the time of the rendition of 
judgment 
of 
conviction 
by 
the 
trial 
court,subject to rules provided for in Article 
39 [Art. 39, RPC, as amended by R.A. 
10159]. 
 
Subsidiary penalty applies only when the 
convict has no property with which to 
meet the ff. pecuniary liabilities: 
 
1. Reparation of the damage caused 
2. Indemnification 
of 
the 
consequential damages 
3. Fine4. 
The 
cost 
of 
the 
proceedings (Article 38, paragraph 3) 
 
A fine, whether imposed as a single or as an 
alternative penalty, should not and cannot be 
reduced or converted into a prison term.  
 
There is no rule for transmutation of the 
amount of a fine into a term of imprisonment. 
(People v. Dacuycuy, G.R. No. 45127, May 
5,1989, 173 SCRA 90,101) 
 
Rate of subsidiary imprisonment per day 
increased to the highest minimum wage rate 
in the NCR at the time of rendition of 
judgment of conviction by the trial court. 
 
RA 10159 increased the rate of subsidiary 
imprisonment for one day to the highest 
minimum wage rate in the National Capital 
Region. The highest minimum wage rate in 
the National Capital Region is P645.00. 
 
Judgment of conviction must impose 
subsidiary imprisonment 
 
An accused cannot be made to undergo 
subsidiary 
imprisonment 
in 
case 
of 
insolvency to pay the fine imposed upon him 
when the subsidiary imprisonment is not 
imposed in the judgment of conviction. 
(Ramos v. Gonong, No. L-42010, August 
31,1961, 72 SCRA 559,565) 
 
8. Execution and Service 
 
Rules as to how and when a penalty is to 
be executed: 
 
1. No penalty shall be executed except by 
final judgment 
 
When Judgment Becomes Final 
 
GR: A judgment becomes final after the 
lapse of the period for perfecting an appeal 
 
XPN: If the defendant has expressly waived 
in writing his right to appeal or when accused 
applied for probation (Sec. 7, Rule 120, 
ROC) 
 


<!-- PAGE 86 -->

 
 
78 
2. Penalties cannot be executed in a form or 
any circumstances/incidents other than that 
prescribed or authorized by law 
 
 
 
a. Community Service – R.A. No. 
11362; A.M. No. 20-06-14-SC 
 
Definition 
 
Community service shall consist of any 
actual physical activity which: 
 
1. Inculcates civic consciousness; 
2. Intended towards the improvement 
of a public work or promotion of a 
public service; 
3. Shall be rendered in the place 
where the crime was committed, 
under such terms as the court shall 
determine. 
 
Offenses Contemplated 
 
It authorizes the court, in its discretion, to 
require community service in lieu of 
imprisonment for offenses punishable by 
arresto menor and arresto mayor 
(Community Service Act, RA 11362). 
 
One-time availment 
 
The privilege of rendering community service 
in lieu of imprisonment shall be availed of 
only once  (Community Service Act, RA 
11362). 
 
Application for Community Service 
 
Applications must be filed within the period to 
perfect an appeal and shall be resolved 
within five days from filing. 
 
Upon receipt of the application: 
 
1. 
Barangay 
chairperson 
or 
authorized representative of the 
barangay where the crime was 
committed 
 
2. Representative from the provincial 
or city’s Probation Office 
 
3. Local government unit’s Social 
Welfare 
Development 
Officer 
(SWDO). 
 
Grant of the Application 
 
Under R.A. No. 11362, also known as the 
Community Service Act, the Court may, in its 
discretion, and lieu of service in jail, require 
that the penalties of arresto menor and 
arresto mayor be served by the defendant by 
rendering community service in the place 
where the crime was committed, and under 
such terms as the court shall determine, 
taking into consideration the gravity of the 
offense and the circumstances of the case. 
(Realiza vs. People, G.R. No. 228745, Aug. 
26, 2020) 
 
Duty of judge after promulgation of 
judgment 
 
All judges concerned, after promulgation of 
judgment or order where the penalty for the 
offense is arresto menor or arresto mayor 
shall: 
 
1. Inform the accused in open court 
that he has an option to apply that the 
penalty be served by rendering 
community service. 
 
3. Explain to the accused that the same 
will be barred to apply for community 
service or probation should he or she 
choose to appeal the conviction. 
 
 
 
 
 


<!-- PAGE 87 -->

 
 
79 
 
 
 
 
b. Recognizance – R.A. No. 10389 
 
Recognizance is a condition in lieu of bail 
under which the accused is released upon 
his promise to appear and answer the 
criminal charge against him whenever 
required. The accused may also be released 
on the recognizance of a responsible person 
in accordance with the Rules. (Sec. 15, Rule 
114, Rules of Court) 
 
Release on Recognizance as a Matter of 
Right 
 
The release on recognizance of any person 
in custody or detention for the commission of 
an offense is a matter of right when the 
offense is not punishable by death, reclusion 
perpetua, or life imprisonment:  
 
Provided, That the accused or any person on 
behalf of the accused files the application for 
such: 
 
(a) Before or after conviction by the 
Metropolitan Trial Court, Municipal 
Trial Court, Municipal Trial Court in 
Cities and Municipal Circuit Trial 
Court; and 
 
(b) Before conviction by the Regional 
Trial Court: Provided, further, That a 
person in custody for a period equal 
to or more than the minimum of the 
principal penalty prescribed for the 
offense charged, without application 
of the Indeterminate Sentence Law, 
or any modifying circumstance, shall 
be 
released 
on 
the 
person’s 
recognizance. (Section 5, R.A. No. 
10389) 
 
 
Disqualifications 
for 
Release 
on 
Recognizance 
  
Any of the following circumstances shall be a 
valid ground for the court to disqualify an 
accused from availing of the benefits 
provided herein: 
 
(a) The accused bad made untruthful 
statements in his/her sworn affidavit 
prescribed under Section 5(a); 
 
 (b) The accused is a recidivist, quasi-
recidivist, habitual delinquent, or has 
committed a crime aggravated by the 
circumstance of reiteration; 
 
 (c) The accused had been found to 
have previously escaped from legal 
confinement, evaded sentence or 
has violated the conditions of bail or 
release on recognizance without 
valid justification; 
 
 (d) The accused had previously 
committed 
a 
crime 
while 
on 
probation, parole or under conditional 
pardon; 
 
 (e) The personal circumstances of 
the accused or nature of the facts 
surrounding his/her case indicate the 
probability of flight if released on 
recognizance; 
 
 (f) There is a great risk that the 
accused may commit another crime 
during the pendency of the case; and 
 
 (g) The accused has a pending 
criminal case which has the same or 
higher penalty to the new crime 
he/she is being accused of.

80 
c. Successive Service of Sentence 
 
GR: When the culprit has to serve 2 or more 
penalties, 
he 
shall 
serve 
them 
simultaneously, if the nature of the penalties 
will permit. 
 
Example:  
 
The following can be simultaneously served 
with one another: 
 
1. Perpetual absolute 
Disqualification 
2. Perpetual special Disqualification 
3. Temporary absolute 
Disqualification 
4. Temporary special Disqualification 
5. Destierro 
6. Suspension 
7. Bond to keep the peace and Fine 
8. Civil interdiction 
9. Confiscation and payment of 
costs 
10. Public censure 
 
All 
of 
the 
above 
can 
be 
served 
simultaneously with imprisonment, except 
destierro [In re: Pete Lagran, G.R. No. 
147270 (2001)]. 
 
XPN: If the nature of the penalties (e.g. 
deprivation of liberty) does not permit 
simultaneous service, the order of their 
respective severity shall be followed [Art. 70, 
RPC]. 
 
Ex. Deprivation of Liberty - Penalties 
consisting in deprivation of liberty 
cannot be served simultaneously by 
reason of the nature of such penalties 
[In re: Pete Lagran, G.R. No. 147270 
(2001)]. 
They 
must 
be 
served 
successively 
 
The respective severity of the penalties shall 
be determined in accordance with the 
following scale: 
 
 
1. Death 
2. Reclusion perpetua 
3. Reclusion temporal 
4. Prision mayor 
5. Prision correccional 
6. Arresto mayor 
7. Arresto menor 
8. Destierro 
9. Perpetual absolute disqualification 
10 Temporary absolute 
disqualification 
11. Suspension from public office, 
the right to vote and be voted for, the 
right to follow a profession or calling 
12. Public censure 
 
d. Three-Fold Rule 
 
The three-fold rule requires the following: 
 
a) 
The maximum duration of the 
convict's sentence shall not be 
more than three-fold the length of 
time corresponding to the most 
severe of the penalties imposed 
upon him. 
 
b) 
Such maximum period shall in 
no case exceed forty years. 
 
c) 
In applying this rule, the 
duration of reclusion perpetua 
shall be computed at thirty years. 
 
 
 
Three-fold Rule Pertains to Service of 
Sentence, not to Imposition of Sentence 
 
This Rule only talks about the maximum 
period of imprisonment to be served by the 
convict (e.g. even if the accused is 
sentenced to suffer 3 reclusion perpetuas, 
there is a limit to the period for his 
imprisonment.) 
 


<!-- PAGE 89 -->

 
 
81 
It has nothing to do with the imposition of 
sentence of the accused. Meaning, the court 
must still impose for as many penalties there 
are. For as long as in the service of convict’s 
sentence, the three-fold rule places a limit on 
his period of imprisonment. 
 
The imposition of sentence is the duty of the 
judge, while the application of the three-fold 
rule is the duty of the warden. 
 
Step-by-step computation of the three-
fold rule 
 
1. List all penalties imposed, per count, 
indicating: 
a. the duration of each divisible 
penalty; 
and 
b. the nature of each indivisible 
penalty. 
2. Identify the most severe penalty 
among those imposed. 
3. Compute three (3) times the duration 
of the most severe penalty. 
If the most severe penalty is reclusion 
perpetua, it shall be computed as thirty (30) 
years for purposes of applying the Three-
Fold Rule. 
4. Add the durations of all the penalties 
imposed. 
5. Compare the results of Steps 3 and 
4. 
6. The convict shall serve the lesser 
period 
between: 
a. the sum of all penalties imposed; 
or 
b. three (3) times the most severe 
penalty, 
provided that in no case shall the 
period to be served exceed forty (40) 
years 
 
 
How to apply the three-fold rule 
 
Step-by-step computation  
 
1. List all penalties imposed, per count, 
indicating: 
 
a. the duration of each 
divisible 
penalty; 
and 
 
b. 
the 
nature 
of 
each 
indivisible penalty. 
 
2. Identify the most severe penalty 
among those imposed. 
 
3. Compute three (3) times the duration 
of the most severe penalty. 
 
If the most severe penalty is reclusion 
perpetua, it shall be computed as thirty 
(30) years for purposes of applying the 
Three-Fold Rule. 
 
Note: Reclusion perpetua is an indivisible 
penalty; and that the duration for this is 
from 20 years and 1 day to 40 years. 
 
However, for purposes of computation 
in the application of the three-fold 
rule, reclusion perpetua, whenever it is 
among the penalties imposed by the 
court, shall be computed at 30 years. 
 
The 30-year period is only for purposes 
of: 
 
a) Determining convict’s eligibility 
for pardon (meaning, after serving 
30 years, the convict is eligible for 
parole) 
 
b) Computing the maximum period 
of imprisonment to be served by the 
accused pursuant to the three-fold 
rule, when at least one of the 
penalties 
imposed 
is 
reclusion 
perpetua. (If reclusion perpetua is 
not even among those imposed by 
the court, then just apply the three-
fold rule, as is.) 
 
4. Add the durations of all the penalties 
imposed. 
 
5. Compare the results of Steps 3 and 
4. 
 
6. The convict shall serve the lesser 
period between: 


<!-- PAGE 90 -->

 
 
82 
 
a. the sum of all penalties 
imposed; or 
 
b. three (3) times the most 
severe penalty, 
provided that in no case 
shall the period to be 
served exceed forty (40) 
years. 
 
 
Example:  
 
Assume: 
 
X committed separate crimes and was 
convicted as follows: 
 
1. Homicide 
Penalty imposed: Reclusion 
temporal 
Duration: 17 years 
 
2. Robbery 
Penalty imposed: Prision mayor 
Duration: 10 years 
 
3. Serious physical injuries 
Penalty imposed: Prision 
correccional 
Duration: 6 years 
 
Step 1: Add all penalties 
 
17 + 10 + 6 = 33 years 
 
Step 2: Identify the most severe penalty 
 
Most severe penalty = Reclusion temporal 
(17 years) 
 
Step 3: Compute three (3) times the most 
severe penalty 
 
17 x 3 = 51 years 
 
Step 4: Compare the results 
 
Total penalties = 33 years 
Three-fold of the most severe penalty  
= 51 years 
 
Step 5: Apply the lesser period 
 
X shall serve 33 years, since it is the lesser 
period and does not exceed 40 years. 
 
 
Subsidiary Imprisonment  
 
The three-fold rule does not preclude 
subsidiary imprisonment for failure to pay a 
fine, provided that the principal penalty does 
not exceed 6 years. The provision of Art. 70 
that "no other penalty to which he may be 
liable shall be inflicted after the sum total of 
those imposed equals the said maximum 
period," only means that the convict shall not 
serve the excess over the maximum of three-
fold the most severe penalty. 
 
He shall serve the penalties simultaneously 
if the nature of the penalties so permits. 
Otherwise, the penalties shall be served 
successively, in the order of their severity, as 
follows: 
 
1. Death 
2. Reclusion perpetua 
3. Reclusion temporal 
4. Prision mayor 
5. Prision correccional 
6. Arresto mayor 
7. Arresto menor 
8. Destierro 
9. Perpetual absolute disqualification 
10. Temporary absolute disqualification 
11. Suspension from public office, the 
right to vote and be voted for, and the 
right to follow a profession or calling 
12. Public censure 
 
(Article 70, Revised Penal Code) 
 
 
 
 
 
 


<!-- PAGE 91 -->

 
 
83 
e. Indeterminate Sentence Law – Act 
No. 4103, as amended 
 
Indeterminate sentence 
 
An indeterminate sentence is a sentence 
with a minimum term and a maximum term 
which the court is mandated to impose for the 
benefit of a guilty person who is not 
disqualified thereof.  
 
Rationale for the imposition of an 
indeterminate sentence 
 
The 
reason 
is 
to 
avoid 
prolonged 
imprisonment because the same is proven to 
be more destructive than constructive to 
offenders.  
 
Minimum and maximum terms 
 
“Minimum term” refers to the duration of the 
sentence which the convict shall serve as a 
minimum to be eligible for parole. 
 
“Maximum term” refers to the maximum 
limit of the duration that the convict may be 
held in jail. For special laws, it is anything 
within the inclusive range of the prescribed 
penalty.  
 
 
 
Mandatory application 
 
Application of ISLAW is mandatory because 
the law employs the phrases “convicts shall 
be sentenced” and “the court shall sentence 
the accused to an indeterminate sentence” 
[People v. Yu Lian, CA 40 OG 4205]. 
 
Applicability 
 
ISLAW is Applicable to those sentenced with 
imprisonment exceeding one (1) year. 
 
Note: The application of ISLAW is 
based 
on 
the 
penalty 
actually 
imposed. If the court imposed a 
penalty of imprisonment of 6 months 
and 1 day of prision correccional, 
ISLAW does not apply. 
 
 
 
 
Disqualifications/Straight Penalty 
 
Even if the sentence exceeds 1 year of 
imprisonment, the following accused are 
disqualified from the availing the benefits of 
ISLAW, meaning, they shall be given a 
STRAIGHT PENALTY (not indeterminate), 
viz: 
 
1. Convicted of Piracy 
 
2. Habitual delinquents (but applies to 
recidivists). 
 
3. Convicted of offenses punished with death 
penalty or Life imprisonment. 
 
However, when there are two mitigating 
circumstances, despite the offense being 
punishable by reclusion temporal to death, 
ISLAW applies. [People v. Roque, 90 Phil 
142, 146 (1951)] 
 
4. Those who shall have escaped from 
confinement or Evaded service of sentence. 
 
5. 
Those 
whose 
maximum 
term 
of 
imprisonment does Not exceed one year. 
 
6. Those sentenced to the penalty of 
Destierro or Suspension. 
 
7. Convicted of misprision of Treason, 
Rebellion, Espionage, Sedition. 
 
8. Convicted of Treason, conspiracy or 
proposal to commit treason. 
 


<!-- PAGE 92 -->

 
 
84 
9. Those who Violated the terms of 
conditional pardon granted to them by the 
Chief Executive. 
 
10. Those who had been sentenced by final 
Judgment at the time of the approval of this 
law. 
 
Rules in determining minimum and 
maximum periods in the Indeterminate 
Sentence 
 
A.  RPC violation 
 
Maximum Term - that which could properly 
imposed under the rules provided by RPC, 
taking 
into 
account 
the 
attending 
circumstances (mitigating, aggravating, etc.) 
 
Minimum Term - that which is anywhere 
within the range of the penalty next lower to 
that prescribed by the RPC. 
 
Note: Prescribed penalty is what the 
penalty is, without looking at the 
circumstances. 
Imposed 
penalty 
takes 
into 
account 
the 
circumstances. 
 
Q: X was convicted of a complex crime of 
direct assault with homicide aggravated 
by the commission of the crime in a place 
where public authorities are engaged in 
the discharge of their duties.  
 
The penalty for direct assault is prision 
correccional in its medium and maximum 
period.  
 
What 
is 
the 
correct 
indeterminate 
penalty? (2012 BAR) 
 
A: 10 years of prision mayor as minimum 
term to 17 years & 4 months of reclusion 
temporal as maximum term. 
 
This is so because 17 years and 4 months is 
the commencement of the duration of the 
maximum period of reclusion temporal while 
10 years is part of prision mayor, the penalty 
next lower in degree to reclusion temporal. 
 
 
 
 
B. Special Penal Law violation 
 
Maximum Term - must not exceed the 
maximum term fixed by said law 
 
Minimum Term - must not be less than the 
minimum term prescribed by the said law 
 
Q: Bruno was charged with homicide for 
killing the 75-year old owner of his 
rooming house. The prosecution proved 
that Bruno stabbed the owner causing his 
death; and that the killing happened at 10 
in the evening in the house where the 
victim and Bruno lived.  
 
Bruno, on the other hand, successfully 
proved that he voluntarily surrendered to 
the authorities; that he pleaded guilty to 
the crime charged; that it was the victim 
who first attacked and did so without any 
provocation on his (Bruno's) part, but he 
prevailed because he managed to draw 
his knife with which he stabbed the 
victim. The penalty for homicide is 
reclusion 
temporal. 
Assuming 
a 
judgment 
of 
conviction 
and 
after 
considering the attendant circumstances, 
what penalty should the judge impose? 
(2013 BAR) 
 
A: Bruno should be sentenced to an 
indeterminate sentence penalty of arresto 
mayor in any of its period as minimum term 
to prision correccional in its medium period 
as maximum term. Bruno was entitled to the 
privileged 
mitigating 
circumstances 
of 
incomplete self-defense and the presence of 
at 
least 
two 
mitigating 
circumstances 
(voluntary surrender and plea of guilt) without 


<!-- PAGE 93 -->

 
 
85 
any aggravating circumstance under Art. 69 
and 63 (5) of the RPC respectively, which 
lowers the prescribed penalty for homicide 
which is reclusion temporal to prision 
correccional. 
 
 
Step 
by 
Step 
Computation 
of 
Indeterminate Penalty (We will use the 
Bruno example above.) 
 
Bruno was able to prove voluntary surrender, 
voluntary plea of guilt, and then we have an 
incomplete self-defense - a privileged 
mitigating circumstance. Thus, we have 2 
ordinary MC, 1 privileged MC, and no AC. 
 
1. Consider first the Privileged Mitigating 
Circumstance 
Whenever there is a privileged mitigating 
circumstance present, apply it first before 
computing the penalty. 
 
In the example, since we have incomplete 
self-defense, 
you 
have 
to 
lower 
the 
prescribed penalty by one degree because it 
is a privileged MC. Thus, the prescribed 
penalty of reclusion temporal will be reduced 
to prision mayor. 
 
2. Consider the Ordinary Mitigating 
Circumstance 
 
Now, there are 2 ordinary MCs, with no AC. 
Pursuant to Art. 64, the penalty shall be 
reduced by one degree. Therefore, the 
penalty of prision mayor is further reduced to 
prision correccional. 
 
3. Determine the Maximum Term, after 
considering 
all 
aggravating 
and 
mitigating circumstances, if any. 
 
In the example, since we have already 
applied all the attending circumstances, then 
we will impose “prision correccional in its 
medium period” as the maximum term. 
 
4 Determine the Minimum Term of the 
Indeterminate Sentence 
 
You go one degree lower than the maximum 
term. Thus, the minimum term should be 
arresto mayor in its medium period (or any 
period in the discretion of the court). 
 
 
f. Probation – P.D. No. 968, as amended 
 
Probation is not a right but a privilege 
subject to the discretion of the court. The 
discretion is exercised primarily for the 
benefit of society as a whole and only 
secondarily for the personal advantage of the 
accused [Amandy v. People, G.R. No. L-
76258 (1988)]. 
The trial court may, after it shall have 
convicted and sentenced a defendant for a 
probationable penalty and upon application 
by said defendant within the period for 
perfecting an appeal, suspend the execution 
of the sentence and place the defendant on 
probation for such period and upon such 
terms and conditions as it may deem best. 
 
Disqualification 
 
The following are disqualified to apply for 
probation: 
 
(a) convicted of any offense against 
the security of the State; 
  
(b) sentenced to serve a maximum 
term of imprisonment of more than 6 
years; 
  
(c) who have been once on 
probation under the provisions of 
this Decree; and 
 
(d) 
who 
have 
previously 
been 
convicted by final judgment of an 
offense punished by imprisonment of 


<!-- PAGE 94 -->

 
 
86 
not less than one month and one day 
and/or a fine of not less than ₱1,000; 
 
(e) 
who 
are 
already 
serving 
sentence. 
 
Mutual Exclusivity of Probation and 
Appeal 
 
 
 
Rules: 
 
a. GR: No application for probation shall be 
entertained or granted if the defendant has 
perfected the appeal from the judgment of 
conviction. The filing of an application for 
probation is deemed a waiver of the right to 
appeal, and vice versa. 
 
b. XPN: When a judgment of conviction 
imposing a non-probationable penalty is 
appealed or reviewed, and such judgment is 
modified through the imposition of a 
probationable penalty, the defendant shall be 
allowed to apply for probation based on the 
modified decision before such decision 
becomes final. 
 
HOWEVER, the accused shall lose the 
benefit of probation should he seek a review 
of the modified decision which already 
imposes a probationable penalty. 
 
The application for probation based on the 
modified decision shall be filed in the trial 
court where the judgment of conviction 
imposing a non-probationable penalty was 
rendered, or in the trial court where such 
case has since been re-raffled. 
 
c. Several Defendants - In a case involving 
several defendants where some have taken 
further appeal, the other defendants may 
apply for probation by submitting a written 
application and attaching thereto a certified 
true copy of the judgment of conviction. 
 
d. Dimakuta Doctrine: 
 
If the decision of the trial court imposed a 
non-probationable penalty, the accused may 
file an appeal solely for the purpose of 
correcting the penalty imposed by the lower 
court, which, when corrected, would entitle 
the accused to apply for probation. 
 
The following must be expressly averred in 
the appeal: 
 
i. 
That 
an 
earlier 
motion 
for 
reconsideration was filed but was 
denied by the trial court; 
 
ii. That the appeal is limited only to a 
review or correction of the penalty 
imposed by the lower court, or that 
the conviction should be for a lesser 
offense which carries a probationable 
penalty; and 
 
iii. That the accused does not seek to review 
the judgment of conviction on the merits, but 
only the penalty, in order to qualify for 
probation. 
 
Other factors to consider in granting the 
application for probation; Reasons for 
denial 
 
The court shall take into consideration the 
following: 
 
i. All information relative to the 
character, antecedents, environment, 
mental, and physical condition of the 
offender; and 
ii. 
Available 
institutional 
and 
community resources. (Sec. 8, P.D. 
No. 968) 
 
Denial of application 
 
The courts will deny the application, even if 
the offender is not disqualified, if: 
 
i. The offender is in need of 
correctional treatment that can be 


<!-- PAGE 95 -->

 
 
87 
provided most effectively by his 
commitment to an institution; or 
 
ii. There is undue risk that during the 
period of probation the offender will 
commit another crime; or 
 
iii. Probation will depreciate the 
seriousness 
of 
the 
offense 
committed. 
 
D. EXTINCTION OF CRIMINAL LIABILITY 
 
Every person criminally liable for a felony 
is also civilly liable. Yet, the dismissal of the 
criminal action does not carry with it the 
extinction of the civil liability where: 
 
(a) the acquittal is based on Reasonable 
doubt as only preponderance of evidence is 
required; 
 
(b) the court declares that the liability of the 
accused is Only civil; and 
 
(c) the civil liability of the accused does Not 
arise from or is not based upon the crime of 
which the accused is acquitted. 
 
1.Total Extinction 
 
a. Death of Convict 
 
Death extinguishes the criminal liability of the 
convict. As to his pecuniary liabilities:  
 
(a) If the convict dies before final 
judgment, his pecuniary liability is 
extinguished; but  
 
(b) If he dies after final judgment, his 
pecuniary liability survives. 
 
b. Service of Sentence 
 
It is a ground for extinction of criminal liability. 
It does not extinguish civil liability. If the 
accused escapes from prison where he was 
serving sentence, the period during which he 
was not in confinement shall be deducted in 
the computation of his total service of 
sentence. 
 
 
 
 
c. Amnesty 
 
It is an act of the sovereign power granting 
oblivion or general pardon for a past offense, 
and is usually granted to a certain class of 
persons who are subject to trial but have not 
yet been convicted. 
Amnesty wipes out not only the penalty but 
also the effects of the criminal liability. But it 
does not extinguish civil liability. 
 
d. Absolute Pardon 
It is the total extinction of the criminal liability 
of the individual to whom it is granted without 
any condition whatsoever resulting in the full 
restoration of his civil rights. 
 
e. Prescription 
 
i. Prescription of Crimes 
 
Prescription of crime refers to the loss of the 
right of the State to prosecute offenders. It 
cannot be waived or extended since it is for 
the benefit of the accused. Once prescription 
has set in, the courts automatically lose 
jurisdiction. 
 
Laws on the Prescription of Crimes 
 
For felonies under the RPC, the governing 
provisions are Articles 90 end 91. 
 
For offenses under special laws, the SPL 
may provide for the period and the rules for 
prescription of violations thereof. 
 
If the Special Penal Law does not so provide, 
Act 3326 “An act to established periods of 
Proscription for Violations Penalized by 
Special Acts and Municipal Ordinances and 
to Provide When Prescription Shall Begin to 
Run” applies. 
 


<!-- PAGE 96 -->

 
 
88 
"Sec. 2. Prescription shall begin to run from 
the day of the commission of the violation of 
the law, and if the same be not known at the 
time, from the discovery thereof and the 
institution of judicial proceedings for its 
investigation and punishment.” 
 
Interruption 
The prescription shall be interrupted when 
the proceedings are instituted against the 
guilty person, and shall begin to run again if 
the proceedings are dismissed for reasons 
not constituting jeopardy” 
 
ii. Act No. 3326 
Act No. 3326 governs the periods of 
prescription for violations penalized by 
special laws and municipal ordinances when 
the law punishing the offense does not itself 
provide a prescriptive period. 
The Act applies to: 
 Violations penalized by special laws, 
and 
 
 Violations of municipal ordinances. 
It does not apply to crimes punishable under 
the Revised Penal Code. 
Periods of prescription 
A. Violations penalized by special laws 
Unless otherwise provided in the special 
law, prescription depends on the penalty 
imposed: 
 
1. One (1) year 
When the offense is punished by 
fine only, or by imprisonment of not 
more than one month, or both. 
 
2. Four (4) years 
When the offense is punished by 
imprisonment of more than one 
month but less than two years. 
 
3. Eight (8) years 
When the offense is punished by 
imprisonment of two years or more 
but less than six years. 
 
4. Twelve (12) years 
When the offense is punished by 
imprisonment of six years or more. 
 
XPN: Treason prescribes after twenty (20) 
years. 
 
 
B. Violations of municipal ordinances - 
prescribe after two (2) months. 
 
Commencement of prescription 
 
Prescription begins to run: 
 
1. From the day of the commission of 
the violation; or 
2. If the violation is not known at the 
time of its commission, from its 
discovery and the institution of 
judicial proceedings for its 
investigation and punishment. 
 
Interruption of prescription 
 
Prescription is interrupted when proceedings 
are instituted against the guilty person. 
 
Prescription shall begin to run again if the 
proceedings are dismissed for reasons not 
constituting double jeopardy. 
 
iii. Prescription of Penalties 
 
1. Death and reclusion perpetua in 20 
years; 
2. Other afflictive penalties (reclusion 
temporal to prision mayor) in 15 
years; 
3. 
Correctional 
penalty 
(prision 
correctional) in 10 years; 
4. Arresto mayor in 5 years; and 
5. Light penalties in 1 year. 
 
 


<!-- PAGE 97 -->

 
 
89 
f. Marriage between the Offender and 
the Offended Party 
 
 
 
The subsequent valid marriage between the 
offender and the offended party shall 
extinguish the criminal action or the penalty 
imposed. 
 
In case it is the legal husband who is the 
offender, the subsequent forgiveness by the 
wife as the offended party shall extinguish 
the criminal action or the penalty: Provided, 
That the crime shall not be extinguished or 
the penalty shall not be abated if the 
marriage is void ab initio. 
 
2.Partial Extinction 
 
a. Conditional Pardon – Act No. 1524 
 
 
The grant of Conditional Pardon is a silent 
contract, a covenant between the President 
and the convict. To be effective, the 
conditional Pardon must be accepted in 
writing by the accused. It is subject to a 
condition which, if complied with by the 
convict could result in his final release but 
which, if not complied with or violated, may 
result to the revocation of the conditional 
pardon. 
 
Requisites for Conditional Pardon 
 
It must be given after final judgment and 
must be accepted because of the conditions 
which must be strictly complied with. For, if 
the offender violates the condition of his 
pardon and the penalty remitted is less than 
six years, he shall be meted an additional 
penalty, viz: 
 
“Art. 159. Other cases of evasion of service 
of sentence. - However, if the penalty 
remitted by the granting of such pardon be 
higher than six years, the convict shall then 
suffer the unexpired portion of his original 
sentence.” 
 
Effects of conditional pardon on civil 
liability 
A conditional pardon does not extinguish the 
civil liability arising from the crime. 
 
Violation of conditional pardon 
 
The court may revoke or continue his 
probation and modify the conditions thereof. 
If revoked, the court shall order the 
probationer to serve the sentence originally 
imposed. An order revoking the grant of 
probation or modifying the terms and  
 
b. Commutation of Sentence 
 
Commutation of sentence is the reduction of 
sentence made by the President in the 
exercise 
of 
his 
pardoning 
power. 
It 
extinguishes criminal liability partially and 
has the effect of changing the penalty to a 
lesser one. It may be in any specific form. 
The fact that the President ordered the 
voluntary release of the prisoner before the 
sentence 
is 
served 
amounts 
to 
a 
commutation of sentence.

Who Grants Time Allowances Under Arts.

1. Director of the Bureau of Corrections

d. Parole 
 
 
 
 
 
Conditions of parole 
 
a. Such prisoner is fitted by his 
training for release; 
 
b. there is reasonable probability that 
such prisoner will live and remain at 
liberty without violating the law; and 
 
c. 
Such 
release 
will 
not 
be 
incompatible with the welfare of 
society 
 
While it is true that a convict may be released 
from prison on parole when he had served 
the minimum period of his sentence; the 
pendency of another criminal case, however, 
is a ground for tine disqualification of such 
convict from being released on parole. 
 
 
E. CIVIL LIABILITY EX DELICTO 
 
Our law states that every person criminally 
liable for a felony is also civilly liable. 
 
This civil liability ex delicto may be recovered 
through a civil action which, under our Rules 
of Court, is deemed instituted with the 
criminal action. This kind of civil liability must 
arise from the criminal act. If not the civil 
liability arises from another source of 
obligation (e.g. contract) then the civil action 
is not deemed instituted with the criminal 
action. 
 
 
1.Primary and Subsidiary 
 
The injured party has two (2) options in 
pursuing the civil liability of the employer for 
the acts of his employee 
 
1) If he chooses to file a civil action for 
damages based on quasi-delict under Article 
2180 and succeeds in proving the negligence 
of the employee, the liability of the employer 
is primary direct and solidary. It is not 
conditioned on the insolvency of the 
employee. 
 
The responsibility of employers for the 
negligence of their employees in the 
performance of their duties is primary, that is, 
the injured party may recover from the 
employers 
directly, 
regardless 
of 
the 
solvency of their employees. 
 
(2) If he chooses to pursue civil action to 
recover civil liability ex-delicto, then liability of 
the employer is only subsidiary. 
 
2.Restitution, Reparation, and 
Indemnification 
 
It is the act of returning to the owner the 
property or the monetary value of loss. 
Restitution of the thing itself must be made 
whenever possible, with allowance for any 
deterioration or diminution of value as 
determined by the court (Art. 105, RPC). 
 
Note: Restitution should apply when 
offense is proven, even if the accused 
is acquitted 
  


<!-- PAGE 100 -->

 
 
92 
GR: The thing itself shall be restored, even 
though it be found in the possession of a third 
person who has acquired it by lawful means. 
 
XPN: In cases where the thing has been 
acquired by the third person in the manner 
and under the requirements which, by law, 
bar an action for its recovery (Art. 105, RPC). 
 
Reparation 
 
The court shall determine the amount of 
damage, taking into consideration the price 
of the thing, whenever possible, and its 
special sentimental value to the injured party, 
and reparation shall be made accordingly 
{Art. 106, RPC). This applies when restitution 
is not possible. 
 
REPARATION = actual value of lost item 
which cannot be recovered + sentimental 
value to original owner 
 
Indemnification 
 
Indemnification for consequential damages 
shall include not only those caused to the 
injured party, but also those suffered by his 
family or by a third person by reason of the 
crime [Art. 107, RPC]. 
 
Indemnity v. Restitution 
Restitution is applicable to crimes against 
property; whereas indemnity is applicable to 
crimes against persons. 
 
3.Civil Liability of an Offender 
Exempted from Criminal Liability 
 
The exemption from criminal liability in favor 
of an imbecile or an insane person, and a 
person under fifteen years of age, or one who 
over fifteen but under eighteen years of age, 
who has acted without discernment, and 
those acting under compulsion of an 
irresistible force or under the impulse of an 
uncontrollable fear of an equal or greater 
injury does not include exemption from civil 
liability. (Article 101, RPC) 
 
4.Share of Each Person Civilly Liable for 
a Felony 
 
If there are two or more persons civilly liable 
for a felony, the courts shall determine the 
amount for which each must respond. (Article 
109, RPC) 
 
5.Preference in Payment 
 
The 
principals, 
accomplices, 
and 
accessories, each within their respective 
class, shall be liable severally (in solidum) 
among themselves for their quotas, and 
subsidiarily for those of the other persons 
liable. The subsidiary liability shall be 
enforced, first against the property of the 
principals; 
next, 
against 
that 
of 
the 
accomplices, and, lastly, against that of the 
accessories. 
 
Whenever the liability in solidum or the 
subsidiary liability has been enforced, the 
person by whom payment has been made 
shall have a right of action against the others 
for the amount of their respective shares. 
 
 
 
 
6.Extinction and Survival of Civil 
Liability Ex Delicto 
 
Any person who has participated gratuitously 
in the proceeds of a felony shall be bound to 
make restitution in an amount equivalent to 
the extent of such participation. 
 
Extinction of civil liability 
  
Civil liability established in Articles 100, 101, 
102, and 103 of this Code shall be 
extinguished in the same manner as 
obligations, 
in 
accordance 
with 
the 
provisions of the Civil Law. (Article 112), 


<!-- PAGE 101 -->

 
 
93 
  
1. Payment or performance; 
2. Loss of the thing due; 
3. Condonation or remission of debt; 
4. Confusion or merger of the rights 
of creditor 
and debtor; 
5. Compensation; and 
6. Novation. 
 
Other 
Causes: 
Annulment, 
rescission, 
fulfillment of a resolutory condition, and 
prescription. (Art. 1231, NCC) 
 
Survival of Civil Liability Ex-Delicto 
  
According to Article 113 of the Revised Penal 
Code, the offender shall continue to be 
obliged to satisfy the civil liability resulting 
from 
the 
crime 
committed 
by 
him, 
notwithstanding the fact that he has served 
his sentence, OR has not been required to 
serve the same by reason of amnesty, 
pardon, commutation of sentence or any 
other reasons. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 


<!-- PAGE 102 -->

 
 
94 
 
III. CRIMES AND THEIR PENALTIES 
RPC Book 2 and Related Special Laws 
 
 
 
A. TITLE ONE 
– CRIMES AGAINST 
NATIONAL SECURITY AND THE LAW 
OF NATIONS 
 
 
I. SECTION ONE. —  TREASON AND 
ESPIONAGE 
 
ART. 114 - TREASON 
 
Elements:  
 
1. That the offender owes Allegiance to the 
Government of the Philippines; (a Filipino 
citizen or Resident alien) 
 
2. There is a War in which the Philippines is 
involved 
 
3. The offender either (modes of committing):  
 
a. Levies war against the government 
 
b. Adheres to the Enemies, giving them 
aid or comfort. 
 
Allegiance 
 
The obligation for fidelity and obedience 
which the individual owes to the government 
under which he lives, or to his sovereign in 
return for the protection he receives (Laurel 
v. Misa, G.R. No. L-409, 1947).  
 
Two (2) Modes of Commission 
 
a. Levying War - Actual assemblage of 
persons for the purpose of executing a 
treasonable design. The war must be against 
the government. 
 
b. Adherence to the enemy and giving of 
aid and comfort. It occurs when a citizen 
intellectually or emotionally favors the enemy 
and harbors sympathy or conviction disloyal 
to his country (Cramer vs. U.S. Supreme 
Court 918) 
 
The "enemies" in Article 114 is a foreign 
country (U.S. v. Magnayon, 3 Phil. 478) for 
this article treats the circumstances of war. It 
cannot refer to rebels covered by Article 135 
which are acts against the government of its 
own citizens. 
 
“Giving aid and comfort” partakes a deed 
or physical activity which may strengthen or 
tend to strengthen the enemy of the 
government or tends to weaken the power of 
the government or the country to resist or 
attack   the enemies. 
 
Place of Commission  
 
a. If by a Filipino citizen – can be committed 
outside of the Philippines.  
 
b. If by an alien – must be committed in the 
Philippines. 
 
Treason is a war crime 
 
 Treason is not an all-time offense. It cannot 
be committed in peacetime. While there is 
peace, there are no traitors.  
 
Two-tiered requirement for Treason 
 
1) Testimony of at least two witnesses 
to the same overt act. (Two-witness 
rule) 
 
  
"Every act, movement, deed, and word of 
the defendant charged to constitute 


<!-- PAGE 103 -->

 
 
95 
treason must be supported by the 
testimony of two witnesses." (Cramer v. 
United States, 65 SUP. Ct., 918.) 
 
2) Confession of guilt in open court. 
 
Treason as a continuing offense 
 
It may be committed by one single act, by a 
series of acts, or by several series thereof, 
not only in a single time, but in different times, 
it being a continuous crime (People v. 
Victoria, G.R. No. L369, 1947). 
 
ART. 115 – CONSPIRACY TO COMMIT 
TREASON  
 
Elements: AgED 
 
It is committed when: 
 
1. In times of war, two or more persons come 
to an Agreement to levy war against the 
government or 
 
2. To adhere to the Enemies and to give 
them aid and comfort 
 
3. They Decide to commit it (Article 115, 
RPC) 
 
Proposal to commit treason is committed, 
when in times of war, a person who has 
decided to levy war against the Government, 
or to adhere to the enemies and to give them 
aid and comfort, proposes its execution to 
some other person. 
 
The punishable act here is conspiracy itself 
or the act of proposing to commit the crime 
of treason.  
 
 
Two-witness rule not applicable 
 
The two-witness rule does not apply to these 
crimes as these are separate and distinct 
offenses from treason itself (US v. Bautista, 
6Phil. 581). 
 
 
ART. 116 – MISPRISION OF TREASON  
 
Elements: KNOC 
 
 
1. The offender must Owe allegiance to the 
government of the Philippines; 
 
2. The offender is Not a foreigner; 
 
3. He has Knowledge of a conspiracy to 
commit 
treason 
against 
the 
said 
government; 
 
4. He Conceals  or fails to disclose the same 
to the authorities in which he resides. 
 
 
 
GR: A person who keeps silent as to what he 
knows about the perpetration of an offense is 
not criminally liable, either as a principal, or 
as an accomplice, or as an accessory. 
 
XPN: Misprision of Treason - The offender  is 
punished as an accessory to treason. (U.S. 
vs. Caballeros, et al, 4 Phil. 350) 
 
 
 


<!-- PAGE 104 -->

 
 
96 
ART. 117 – ESPIONAGE  
 
2 Modes of Commission 
 
Elements of 1st Mode: 
 
1) The offender without any authority Enters 
a 
warship, 
fort, 
naval 
or 
military 
establishment or reservation; 
 
2) He 
Obtains 
information, 
plans, 
photographs or other data of confidential 
nature relative to the defense of the 
Philippines. 
 
Elements of 2nd Mode: 
 
1) The offender is Public officer 
 
2) He has in his possession the Articles, 
data or information referred to in the first 
mode of committing this crime; and  
 
3) He Discloses their contents to a 
representative of a foreign nation.  (Art. 
117, par 2) 
 
 
II. SECTION TWO. — PROVOKING WAR 
AND DISLOYALTY IN CASE OF WAR 
 
ART. 118 — INCITING TO WAR OR GIVING 
MOTIVES FOR REPRISALS  
 
ELEMENTS: UP-EX 
 
1. Committed by any public officer or 
employee, and any private individual by 
Unlawful or unauthorized acts which: 
 
2. a. Provoke or give occasion for a war 
involving 
or 
liable 
to 
involve 
the 
Philippines; or  
b. Exposure of Filipino citizens to reprisals 
on their persons or property  
 
 
ART. 119 — VIOLATION OF NEUTRALITY 
 
Elements: WIV 
 
1) There is War in which the Philippines is not 
involved  
 
2) Competent authorities have Issued 
regulations to enforce neutrality; and  
 
3) The offender Violates any of said 
regulations. 
 
 
ART. 120 — CORRESPONDENCE WITH 
HOSTILE COUNTRY  
 
It is committed by any person who, in time of 
war, shall have correspondence with an 
enemy country or territory occupied by 
enemy troops: 
 
1. If the correspondence has been prohibited 
by the Government; 
 
2. If such correspondence be carried on in 
ciphers or conventional signs; 
 
3. If notice or information be given thereby 
which might be useful to the enemy; or 
 
4.  If the offender intended to aid the enemy 
by giving such notice or information. 
 
 
ART. 121 — FLIGHT TO ENEMY’S 
COUNTRY  
 
Elements:  FAE 
 
(a) Existence of war in which the Philippines 
is involved;  
(b) The offender owes Allegiance to the 
Philippines; and  
(c) He attempts to Flee to enemy country 
which is prohibited by the government. 
 


<!-- PAGE 105 -->

 
 
97 
Notes: 
 
1. The mere attempt to flee is punished by 
law. 
2. This felony may also be committed by a 
foreigner as he owes allegiance to the 
government even though temporary in 
nature. 
 
 
III. SECTION THREE. — PIRACY AND 
MUTINY ON THE HIGH SEAS 
 
ART. 122 — PIRACY IN GENERAL AND 
MUTINY ON THE HIGH SEAS. 
 
Elements: 
 
1. Any person who, on the high seas, shall 
attack or seize a vessel 
 
2. Not being a member of its complement nor 
a passenger (strangers to the vessel) 
 
Persons who smuggled themselves into the 
crew are considered “strangers.” 
 
3. Shall seize the whole or part of the cargo 
of said vessel, its equipment, or personal 
belongings of its complement or passengers. 
 
Privacy v. Mutiny 
 
Piracy 
Mutiny 
 
Persons who attack 
the vessel or seize 
its 
cargo 
are 
strangers to said 
vessels 
 
Persons who attack the 
vessel or seize its cargo 
are members of the 
crew or passengers 
ART. 123 — QUALIFIED PIRACY 
 
If any of the circumstances in Article 123 is 
present, piracy is qualified. The crime is 
Qualified Piracy. 
 
Qualifying Circumstances  
 
1. Whenever the vessel is seized by 
Boarding or Firing upon the same;  
 
2. Whenever the pirates have Abandoned 
their victims without means of saving 
themselves; or  
 
3. Whenever the crime is accompanied by 
Murder, Homicide, Physical injuries or 
Rape. 
 
No complex crime 
 
When any of the crimes of murder, homicide, 
rape, physical injuries accompany piracy, 
there is no complex crime. Instead, there is 
only one crime committed - qualified piracy. 
 
Article 123 specifies that it applies to any of 
the crimes in the "preceding article." Thus, it 
embraces not only qualified piracy but 
also qualified mutiny notwithstanding its 
heading. However, the first circumstance 
(Whenever the vessel is seized by Boarding 
or Firing upon the same) applies only to 
piracy because in mutiny the offenders are 
"insiders" of the vessel. 
 
Aiding pirates or abetting piracy 
 
Any person who knowingly and in any 
manner aids or protects pirates, such as 
giving them information about the movement 
of police or other peace officers of the 
government, or acquires or receives property 
taken by the pirates or in any manner derives 
and benefits therefrom; or any person who 


<!-- PAGE 106 -->

 
 
98 
directly or indirectly abets the commission of 
piracy, shall be punished as an accomplice 
of the principal offenders. 
 
 
1. Piracy – P.D. No. 532 
 
Definition of Terms (Sec. 2 of PD 532) 
 
a. Philippine Waters refer to all bodies of 
water, such as but not limited to, seas, gulfs, 
bays around, between and connecting each 
of the Islands of the Philippine Archipelago, 
irrespective of its depth, breadth, length or 
dimension, and all other waters belonging to 
the Philippines by historic or legal title, 
including territorial sea, the sea-bed, the 
insular shelves, and other submarine areas 
over which the Philippines has sovereignty or 
jurisdiction. 
 
b. Vessel refers to any vessel or watercraft 
used for transport of passengers and cargo 
from one place to another through Philippine 
Waters. It shall include all kinds and types of 
vessels or boats used in fishing. 
 
c. Piracy refers to any attack upon or seizure 
of any vessel, or the taking away of the whole 
or part thereof or its cargo, equipment, or the 
personal belongings of its complement or 
passengers, irrespective of the value thereof, 
by means of violence against or intimidation 
of persons or force upon things, committed 
by any person, including a passenger or 
member of the complement of said vessel, in 
Philippine waters, shall be considered as 
piracy.  
 
Qualified Piracy 
 
a) If physical injuries or other crimes are 
committed as a result or on the occasion 
thereof, the penalty of reclusion perpetua 
shall be imposed.  
 
b) If 
rape, 
murder 
or 
homicide 
is 
committed as a result or on the occasion 
of 
piracy, 
or 
when 
the 
offenders 
abandoned the victims without means of 
saving themselves, or when the seizure 
is accomplished by firing upon or 
boarding a vessel, the mandatory penalty 
of death shall be imposed. 
 
Aiding pirates or abetting piracy 
Any person who knowingly and in any 
manner aids or protects pirates, such as 
giving them information about the movement 
of police or other peace officers of the 
government, or acquires or receives property 
taken by such pirates or brigands or in any 
manner derives any benefit therefrom; or any 
person who directly or indirectly abets the 
commission of piracy, shall be considered as 
an accomplice of the principal offenders and 
be punished in accordance with the Rules 
prescribed by the Revised Penal Code. 
 
Piracy under the RPC 
Piracy under P.D. 532 
As to where committed 
Can be 
committed 
while the vessel is on 
high 
seas 
or 
in 
Philippine waters 
Can be committed 
only when the vessel 
is in Philippine waters 
As to who may commit 
Can 
only 
be 
committed by persons 
who are not members 
of 
the 
vessel’s 
complement, or the 
passengers 
of 
the 
vessel (strangers) 
Can be committed by 
any person, including 
the 
vessel’s 
complement, or the 
passengers 
of 
the 
vessel 
 
Note: there is no piracy when members of the 
vessel’s complement or its passengers 
attack or seize the vessel or its cargo on high 
seas. The offense would then be theft or 
robbery cognizable by Philippine courts, if 
the crime is committed on a Philippine ship, 
pursuant to Art. 2(1) of the RPC.
